## Page 3


Abstract
This work studies spectral properties of Schr¨odinger operators in the context of aperi-
odic order, using weighted Delone sets to explore the interplay between the underlying
dynamics and spectral properties. We study parameter-dependent families interpolating
between periodic and aperiodic regimes, whose spectra form so-called spectral butterflies.
These reflect fractal and self-similar structures of the spectra.
We review existing results, introduce additional examples, and establish new connections
between works in the literature. The framework is largely dimension-independent and
extends to non-Abelian groups and more general settings.
Zusammenfassung
Diese Arbeit studiert spektrale Eigenschaften von Schr¨odingeroperatoren im Kontext ape-
riodischer Ordnung, wobei gewichtete Delone-Mengen verwendet werden, um das Zusam-
menspiel zwischen der zugrundeliegenden Dynamik und den Spektraleigenschaften zu un-
tersuchen. Wir studieren parameterabh¨angige Familien, die zwischen periodischen und
aperiodischen dynamischen Systemen interpolieren, deren Spektren sogenannte spektrale
Schmetterlinge bilden. Diese spiegeln fraktale und selbst¨ahnliche Strukturen der Spektren
wider.
Wir fassen bestehende Resultate zusammen, f¨uhren zus¨atzliche Beispiele ein und stellen
neue Verbindungen zwischen Arbeiten in der Literatur her.
Der entwickelte Rahmen
ist weitgehend dimensionsunabh¨angig und erstreckt sich auf nicht-abelsche Gruppen und
allgemeinere Situationen.

Introduction
This work is devoted to the spectral analysis of Schr¨odinger-type operators in both discrete
and continuous settings. It is based on a series of joint works [28, 38, 40, 33, 34, 32, 18, 35,
41, 19, 16, 31], and aims to develop a unified framework for models arising in the context
of aperiodic order, in particular those motivated by the theory of quasicrystals. These
models are built either from Delone sets or symbolic dynamical systems, and a central
goal is to understand the spectral properties of the associated operators via suitable
approximations.
This leads naturally to the study of weighted Delone sets and their
associated dynamical systems.
Figure 1.1: The Hofstadter butterfly taken from [125].
Many of the models under consideration depend on parameters that interpolate between
periodic and non-periodic dynamical systems. For periodic configurations, the spectrum of
the corresponding operator can be computed numerically. Plotting the spectrum across
a range of parameters reveals striking internal structures that suggest fractal or self-
similar properties. The most well-known example is the Hofstadter butterfly (Figure 1.1),
associated with the almost Mathieu operator and closely related to the integer quantum
Hall effect [240]. Numerous other spectral ”butterflies” have been discovered [191, 243,
101, 24, 25, 119, 21, 74, 187], each encoding complex spectral behavior.
1


## Page 10


2
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
A second prominent example is the Kohmoto butterfly [150, 191], see Figure 1.2. It is
connected to Sturmian dynamical systems and serves as a model for one-dimensional
quasicrystals. In all these cases, a central question arises: How much spectral informa-
tion about the aperiodic system is encoded in these periodic approximations? In other
words, what can these ”butterflies” tell us about the spectral properties of the underlying
aperiodic models?

Figure 1.2: The Kohmoto butterfly taken from [55].
Several contributions addressing these questions are discussed here. We introduce the
relevant concepts, establish connections to other results in the literature, and explain how
the dynamical structure governs particular spectral behavior. While many of the examples
considered are one-dimensional, the results themselves are often dimension-independent
and extend to broader settings even to an ambient non-Abelian group.
The following three questions form the central thematic focus of this Habilitation thesis:
(A) Do there exist strictly ergodic and aperiodic dynamical systems beyond the Abelian
setting?
(B) Which spectral properties are governed by the underlying structure – such as geom-
etry, combinatorics, or dynamics?
(C) Are all spectral gaps that are predicted by the underlying structure actually there?
We briefly outline the central themes and methods addressed in this thesis in connec-
tion with the above questions. A more detailed discussion and outlook is provided in
Chapters 3 to 7.


## Page 11


3
In the study of aperiodic order, two principal classes of (weighted) Delone sets are com-
monly considered: cut-and-project sets and substitution systems. Some of the most promi-
nent examples – such as the Penrose tiling or the Fibonacci sequence – can be described
within both frameworks. In particular, the Sturmian dynamical systems mentioned earlier
are one-dimensional cut-and-project sets parametrized by an irrational frequency α and
can, under suitable conditions on the continued fraction expansion of α, also be described
via substitutions; see [112, 11].
Substitution-based models are of particular interest in the spectral analysis of associated
Schr¨odinger operators. In various one-dimensional cases, the substitution structure has
been used to construct periodic approximations by iterating the substitution map to a
fixed periodic configuration.
The standard literature on aperiodic order primarily focuses on dynamical systems over
Abelian groups. However, recent developments have extended the construction of cut-and-
project sets to certain non-Abelian groups, giving rise to so-called approximate lattices.
These are studied in the context of geometric group theory; see [58, 59, 60]. Such con-
structions yield point sets D – often Delone sets – in a group G, where G acts freely on
D; that is, the stabilizer of D is trivial. These sets are referred to as aperiodic, and the
associated orbit closure (also known as the hull) defines a dynamical system.
Of particular interest are dynamical systems that are both minimal (every orbit is dense)
and uniquely ergodic (there exists a unique G-invariant probability measure on the hull).
Systems satisfying both properties are called strictly ergodic.
Specific cut-and-project
models constructed in [59] are strictly ergodic. Motivated also by spectral applications and
the central question (A), one aims to identify substitution systems beyond the Abelian
setting that define strictly ergodic dynamical systems.
In the Abelian setting, primitive substitutions yield linearly repetitive configurations. For
G = Rd, the class of linearly repetitive Delone sets is strictly ergodic; see [105, 156]. A
Delone set is called linearly repetitive if there exists a constant C > 0 such that every
patch of D supported in a translated ball of radius r occurs in every patch supported in
any translated ball of radius Cr.
However, when extending the analysis to general amenable and unimodular groups – such
as the lamplighter group – balls may grow exponentially and therefore fail to form a strong
Følner sequence. This therefore requires a metric-free criterion. To this end, the notion
of tempered repetitive weighted Delone sets was introduced in the joint work [35]. It is
shown there that the associated dynamical systems are strictly ergodic for a broad class
of ambient groups. The proof draws on tools from ergodic theory, including the refined
quasi-tiling techniques of Ornstein and Weiss [188, 202]. These results generalize earlier
work not only by encompassing general amenable groups and tempered repetitivity, but
also by admitting weighted Delone sets that do not necessarily have finite local complexity;
see Section 3.3.
While linearly repetitive structures have been thoroughly studied in Abelian settings, non-
trivial examples in non-Abelian groups were, until recently, not available. This gap has
been addressed in the joint work [34], where the classical theory is extended to substitution
systems on homogeneous Lie groups (Section 3.6).
Initial attempts to generalize substitutions from the Abelian setting to the Heisenberg
group – by exploiting its representation in R3 – were unsuccessful. The main obstacle
lies in the geometric distortion that occurs when shifting geometric objects, such as cubes
or balls, in the Heisenberg group.
As illustrated in Example 3.49, such translations
deform the shape in ways that are difficult to control. This challenge was overcome by
introducing a systematic approach: substitutions are now defined in terms of a dilation
datum and a substitution datum that respects the underlying group structure.
This


## Page 12


4
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
framework enabled the construction of a broad class of strictly ergodic and aperiodic
(weighted) Delone sets beyond the Abelian setting, revealing new structural phenomena.
A concrete example of a substitution system on the Heisenberg group is presented in [34];
see also Example 3.49. Notably, the same substitution framework also encompasses many
classical Abelian substitution systems (Section 3.5).
At this point, the connection between symbolic dynamical systems and Delone sets be-
comes central. Both classes of dynamical systems have been extensively studied in the
literature, and the notion of weighted Delone sets provides a unifying framework for them.
This is elaborated in Section 3.2, where we introduce a metric structure suitable for spec-
tral applications discussed below. Although it is folklore that the space of (weighted)
Delone sets can be equipped with a metric, we construct a specific metric under which
the group action becomes Lipschitz continuous (Definition 3.1). The construction is out-
lined in Section 3.2, and full details are deferred to Appendix 8.1. This framework further
allows us to characterize convergence of the underlying dynamical systems in terms of
convergence of patches for general weighted Delone sets, see Corollary 3.25.
Within the author’s PhD, the convergence of dynamical systems was characterized in
terms of the convergence of spectra of associated operators. This was established in the
joint work [29] and further extended to the setting of groupoid C∗-algebras. The proof
relied on C∗-algebraic techniques and is particularly notable as it allows one to reduce
spectral convergence to the convergence of the underlying dynamical structure.
This naturally leads to the central question (B), under which we explore several key
insights throughout this work.
In the joint work [40], we proved that the convergence of dynamical systems also induces
semicontinuity properties for associated invariant measures. In particular, this implies the
convergence of the density of states and the autocorrelation measure, provided the limiting
system is uniquely ergodic (see Section 3.1). One of the main objectives of that work
was to explore periodic approximations in higher dimensions, with a particular emphasis
on cut-and-project sets. This was achieved by smoothing the characteristic function of
the corresponding window.
As a result, the convergence of the underlying dynamical
systems also entails the convergence of relevant measure-theoretic quantities. It is worth
emphasizing that the underlying geometry and combinatorics of the configurations are
incorporated into the notion of dynamical convergence.
Having the spectral characterization in mind, a natural question arises: do periodic ap-
proximations exist, and if so, how fast do they converge? A review of existing spectral
results shows that, for substitution systems, a common approach is to iterate the substi-
tution rule on a fixed periodic configuration. Sufficient criteria for spectral convergence in
the Hausdorff metric have been established for one-dimensional substitution systems [30]
and for block substitutions on Zd [26].
In the joint work [19], we address the general question of when the iterative application of
a substitution to a configuration leads to convergence of the associated dynamical systems
(see Section 3.6). Building on the substitution framework developed in [34], we establish a
general characterization of convergence in terms of so-called substitution graphs. Notably,
we provide a necessary and sufficient condition for the convergence based on the structure
of these graphs.
A key advantage of this approach is that convergence can be verified algorithmically
by analyzing these finite graphs. Moreover, we establish explicit convergence rates for
the dynamical systems (Theorem 3.55). Combined with recent results [28, 41] discussed
below, this also yields exponential convergence rates for the spectra (see Theorem 4.8).


## Page 13


5
Although the framework is formulated in symbolic terms, the underlying methods can be
adapted to specific Delone sets. This approach is illustrated by the case of the octagonal
tiling, as discussed in Section 3.7.
In an ongoing project [20], the above characterization is used to demonstrate that certain
natural choices of periodic approximations may fail to converge. In contrast to the one-
dimensional case, new phenomena arise in higher dimensions, where such poorly chosen
approximations can introduce spectral defects that alter both the essential spectrum and
its Lebesgue measure.
This particularly shows that a more careful choice of periodic
approximations is necessary starting from dimension two.
To study such spectral consequences, we focus on dynamically-defined operators (Chap-
ter 4). Section 4.1 discusses the joint works [28, 41] proving particular spectral estimates.
More precisely, we established qualitative estimates for the Hausdorff distance between
spectra in terms of the distance between the underlying dynamical systems. While spec-
tral convergence was already known from earlier results [29] using C∗-algebraic techniques,
these methods did not provide access to explicit convergence rates.
The works [28] and [41] approach this problem from entirely different techniques. The for-
mer focuses on symbolic dynamical systems and adapts techniques from [79, 80], including
Lipschitz partitions of unity tailored to regions where patches coincide, combined with
resolvent estimates. In contrast, [41] introduces a unifying method based on approximate
eigenfunctions. This approach encompasses classical examples such as the almost Math-
ieu operator, the Kohmoto model, limit periodic potentials and Schr¨odinger operators
assigned with skew-shifts.
Moreover, [41] provides new conceptual insight into the origin of the observed square-root
behavior in the spectral estimates. Specifically, it stems from the interplay between the
amenability of the underlying group and the regularity of the operator coefficients, see
Remark 8.15. It is worth emphasizing that the qualitative behavior of these estimates is
optimal, as demonstrated in the context of the Hofstadter butterfly [205] and the Kohmoto
butterfly [31].
These results apply to a broad class of operators and recover known estimates, such as
those for the Hofstadter butterfly [8] (Section 4.3). Moreover, they lead to new spectral
bounds for substitution systems on homogeneous Lie groups (Section 3.6, based on the
joint work [19]), for the Kohmoto butterfly (Section 4.5, based on the joint work [31]),
and for the unitary almost Mathieu operator (Section 4.4). For the latter, minor gen-
eralizations of the proof in [41] are required; these are provided in Appendix 8.3 for
completeness.
These spectral approximation results also allow for more refined structural insights. For
instance, the analysis of spectral defects in the Kohmoto butterfly, as presented in Sec-
tion 4.5, is based on [31]. Numerical simulations in Figure 1.2 suggest that such defects
occur at rational frequencies; see also Figure 4.3 on page 68. This was originally pointed
out in [51, Rem. 3]. A rigorous proof of this observation is provided in [31]. The result
relies not only on spectral convergence but also on a symbolic encoding of the periodic
approximations in the Kohmoto model, discussed further below.
While the main examples discussed so far are one-dimensional, the underlying methods
extend beyond this setting. In particular, they apply to more general groups such as the
lamplighter group (see Section 4.6), an amenable group with exponential growth. The
details for proving the corresponding spectral estimates – using the methods of [41] – are
provided in Appendix 8.4.
The previous discussion relates directly to the theme of question (B). Moreover, we
investigated criteria in the joint works [38, 32] linking the spectrum of an operator, viewed
as a set, to the growth properties of its generalized eigenfunctions. These results apply to


## Page 14


6
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
both continuous and discrete settings, including operators defined on Delone graphs; see
Section 6.1. A key motivation was the conjecture posed in [102, Conj. 9.9], which asks
whether a generalized eigenfunction bounded by the Agmon ground state implies that the
corresponding eigenvalue lies in the spectrum. This question was affirmatively answered
in [38].
This result is closely related to so-called Shnol’-type theorems [220, 222], where the growth
of generalized eigenfunctions is controlled by the underlying geometry. In [32], we provided
a unified treatment of both perspectives, combining them via a Weyl sequence argument,
suitable cut-off functions, and Caccioppoli-type estimates.
In [33], we also studied the essential spectrum of Schr¨odinger operators on graphs. Under
appropriate geometric conditions, we proved that the essential spectrum coincides with
the set of generalized eigenvalues corresponding to bounded generalized eigenfunctions,
using again a Shnol’-type argument; see Section 6.2.
We now turn to the final central question (C), which is discussed in detail in Chapter 5
and brings us back to the spectral butterflies introduced at the beginning. Building on
the previous developments, a natural question arises: How can the spectral approximation
schemes discussed above be applied to analyze the spectral properties of the corresponding
operators in the aperiodic regime? Furthermore, what insights do these methods offer
into the structure of the associated butterflies? As previously indicated, the dynamical
approach enables new structural results for the Kohmoto butterfly.
One is particularly interested in the aperiodic systems within the Kohmoto model namely,
those operators associated with an irrational frequency. These operators are also referred
to as Sturmian Hamiltonians, since the underlying dynamical systems are Sturmian when-
ever the frequency is irrational.
In the joint work [18], periodic approximations of irrational frequencies, together with
a symbolic encoding of the spectral bands (see Section 5.1), are used to represent the
spectrum as the boundary of a spectral tree (see Section 5.2). This construction is in-
spired by a coding scheme introduced by Raymond [207] for large coupling constants that
control the amplitude of the potential. His approach was revisited and extended in [16],
incorporating recent advances from [18]. A key achievement of [18] was the extension of
this symbolic encoding to all non-zero coupling constants.
Together with the spectral tree representation, these developments ultimately lead to a
resolution of the dry ten Martini problem (DTMP) for Sturmian Hamiltonians, as detailed
in Section 5.3. The DTMP originated in the study of the almost Mathieu operator (the
Hofstadter butterfly) and was addressed in 1981 by Kac: Are all spectral gaps there?
Over the past decades, this question has been extended to other models, including the
Sturmian Hamiltonians. A more comprehensive historical and mathematical account is
provided in Chapter 5.
Spectral gaps are the connected components of the complement of the spectrum of an
operator – visually represented as white regions on each horizontal line in the Kohmoto
or Hofstadter butterfly. The set of spectral gaps is countable, and they can be indexed via
the associated integrated density of states. In the case of dynamically-defined operators,
the underlying dynamical system determines the set of admissible gap labels, as predicted
by the Gap Labeling Theorem. With this in mind, Kac’s question can be reformulated as
follows: For a fixed irrational frequency, does there exist a spectral gap in the spectrum
for each admissible gap label?
Significant progress towards resolving the original DTMP for the almost Mathieu operator
has been made over the past decades [78, 203, 4, 178], and most recently by Avila, You
and Zhou in [6]. For the Sturmian Hamiltonians, the DTMP remained open for more
than 30 years. A first major advance was achieved by Raymond [207], who proved that


## Page 15

We use the same color scheme as in [190].
all gaps are open for sufficiently large coupling constants. For the special case where the
frequency equals the inverse of the golden ratio, Damanik and Gorodetski [92] showed
that all predicted gaps are open for sufficiently small couplings; this model is known
as the Fibonacci Hamiltonian. Mei [183] extended this result to irrational frequencies
with eventually periodic continued fraction expansions. Later, Damanik, Gorodetski, and
Yessen [94] demonstrated – among other remarkable results – that all spectral gaps are
open in the Fibonacci case. All of these frequencies satisfy a Diophantine condition, and
it remained unclear at that stage how to treat general irrational frequencies beyond this
class.
A central difficulty arises in the small coupling regime, where the kinetic term dominates
the potential, and spectral bands begin to overlap. In this setting, it was unclear whether
a symbolic encoding of the spectrum was still feasible, or how to treat general irrational
frequencies in the context of the DTMP. Both challenges were overcome in the joint
work [18], which provided a complete resolution of the DTMP for Sturmian Hamiltonians.
Key innovations in that work include:
• a simultaneous analysis of the space of finite continued fraction expansions, rather
than focusing on a single irrational frequency;
• the use of eigenvalue interlacing arguments to control the positions and overlaps of
spectral bands.
It is worth emphasizing the consequences of this result: no matter how small the amplitude
of the potential term is, all spectral gaps open instantaneously as soon as the amplitude


## Page 16


8
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
becomes nonzero. As a result, the spectrum becomes a Cantor set. In contrast, when the
amplitude vanishes, the spectrum is an interval, and the associated spectral measure is
purely absolutely continuous.
The topological indices of the almost Mathieu operator were used to color the Hofstadter
butterfly in [190]. Adopting a similar strategy, we assigned colors to the spectral gaps
of the Kohmoto butterfly according to their gap indices, as shown in Figure 1.3. This
visualization complements Figure 1.2, where the spectral bands are displayed in black.
Further analysis specific to the Kohmoto model is needed and is carried out in the ongoing
project [15].
Finally, let us emphasize that the new methods developed in [18] open the door to further
investigations into the fractal nature of the Kohmoto butterfly. In a joint project with
Ram Band and Yannik Thomas, we explore whether the Kohmoto butterfly exhibits self-
similarity, using the spectral tree as a central tool. As noted at the beginning of this
introduction, such questions lie at the heart of the study of spectral butterflies. Similar
questions have also been studied in the physics literature, for instance in the context of
the Hofstadter butterfly, see e.g. [216, 215].
A more detailed discussion and outlook of the previously discussed material is provided
in Chapters 3 to 7.
The following joint works form the basis of this Habilitation thesis, which are provided in
the Chapter 2. In each of them, the contributions were equally shared among the authors.

The dynamical and ergodic theoretical per-
spective
Throughout this work, G denotes a locally compact, second countable, Hausdorff (lcsc)
group. For g, h ∈G, gh denotes the group multiplication of the two elements. Further,
e ∈G is the neutral element, i.e. eg = ge = g for all g ∈G. Then g−1 denotes the inverse
of g ∈G, namely gg−1 = g−1g = e. Following [81, Prop. 2.A.9, Thm. 2.B.4] (as well as a
discussion in [35, Sec. 2.1]), there exists a metric dG : G × G →[0, ∞) on the lcsc group
G that is
• continuous (and so dG generates the topology of G),
• left-invariant, i.e. dG(gh1, gh2) = dG(h1, h2) for g, h1, h2 ∈G,
• proper, i.e. closed balls B(g, r) := {h ∈G : dG(g, h) ≤r} of radius r ≥0 and center
g ∈G are compact,
• locally bounded, i.e. every g ∈G admits a neighborhood with finite diameter with
respect to dG.
A metric dG satisyfing the previous conditions is called adapted. We denote by
B(g, r) := {h ∈G : dG(g, h) < r}
the open ball of radius r ≥0 and center g ∈G. Furthermore, let B(r) := B(e, r) and
B(r) := B(e, r).
The central object in this work are continuous group actions on a compact metric space
– so-called topological dynamical systems – and the associated operators, see Chapter 4
for details. A (topological) dynamical system is a triple (Z, G, τ) where
• Z is a compact metric space with metric d,
• G is an lcsc group and
• τ : G × Z →Z is a left-continuous action of the group G on Z, namely for all x ∈Z
and g, h ∈G,
τ(e, x) = x
and
τ
 g, τ(h, x)

= τ(gh, x).
Whenever the action is clear from the context, we use the short notation gx = τ(g, x) for
g ∈G and x ∈Z. Like in groups B(x, r) = {y ∈Z : d(x, y) < r} is the open ball in Z
and B(x, r) = {y ∈Z : d(x, y) ≤r} the closed ball.
In general it is enough to know that the group action on Z is continuous. For the spectral
application, discussed in Chapter 4, one often requires more. For this purpose so-called
Lipschitz-continuous actions are of particular interest [41].

CHAPTER 3. THE DYNAMICAL AND ERGODIC THEORETICAL PERSPECTIVE
Definition 3.1. Let (Z, G, τ) be a topological dynamical system and d be a metric on Z.
Then the dynamical system is called Lipschitz continuous if there exists a constant Cτ > 0
such that
d(gx, gy) ≤
 CτdG(e, g) + 1

d(x, y),
for all x, y ∈Z, g ∈G.
In particular, for fixed g ∈G, the map τ(g, ·) : Z →Z is Lipschitz continuous, and we
require that its Lipschitz constant grows at most linearly with the distance of g from the
identity element e ∈G. Note that Cτ > 0 is independent of x, y ∈Z and g ∈G. This
assumption is satisfied in most examples considered in this work, see Proposition 3.16.
The restriction to linear growth is mainly a practical one, as it facilitates explicit estimates
of spectral distances. A relaxation of this condition – such as sublinear growth or merely
H¨older continuity of the group action – will lead to modified, typically weaker, spectral
estimates; see e.g. [41, Thm. 2.4].
A subset Y ⊆Z is called (G-)invariant if gY := {gx : x ∈Y } ⊆Y for all g ∈G.
Particular examples are the orbits of an element x ∈Y , namely
Orb(x) := {gx : g ∈G} .
It is straightforward to check that the orbit closure Y := Orb(x) ⊆Z is G-invariant, closed
(and so compact as Z is compact) and non-empty. Thus, (Y, G, τ) is again a topological
dynamical system (where τ is restricted to Y ) – a dynamical subsystem of (Z, G, τ).
A central object in this work is the set S of all dynamical subsystems of (Z, G, τ) is
defined by
S := {Y ⊆Z : Y invariant, closed and non-empty} .
As discussed before, we have Orb(x) ∈S for every x ∈Z. However, there might be more
elements in S that are not equal to the orbit closure of a single element x ∈Z.
Clearly, S ⊆K(Z) := {K ⊆Z compact} and the latter is naturally equipped with the
Hausdorff metric [71, 241, 128] defined by
δH : K(Z) × K(Z) →[0, ∞),
δH(X, Y ) := max{sup
x∈X
d(x, Y ), sup
y∈Y
d(y, X)},
(3.1)
where d(x, Y ) := infy∈Y d(x, y) denotes the distance from the point x to the compact set
Y . Since Z is compact, the Hausdorff topology coincides with the Chabauty-Fell topology
[76, 109]. For a detailed discussion of this equivalence, we refer the reader to [26, Sec. 2.2]
and references therein.
Proposition 3.2 ([29], Prop. 4). Let (Z, G, τ) be topological dynamical system and δH be
the Hausdorff metric on K(Z) induced by the metric d on Z. Then (S , δH) is a compact
metric space.
In [26, 29, 30], it was discovered that equipping S with the induced Hausdorff metric, the
convergence of dynamical subsystems implies the convergence of the associated spectra.
In fact, it turns out that this convergence of dynamical systems is equivalent to the re-
quirement that a suitable class of associated operators has spectrum varying continuously
under continuous changes of the underlying dynamical system. This relationship is made
more precise in Section 4.1 and forms the starting point of the present work.
Building on this, we show that
• the Hausdorff distance of spectra is bounded by the distance between the corre-
sponding dynamical systems, see Section 4.1 based on [28, 41];


## Page 21


13
• associated measure-theoretic quantities converge, see Section 3.1 based on [40];
• this framework can be used to construct so-called periodic approximations (Sec-
tion 3.6 based on [19]), which in turn allow one to analyze spectral properties of
more complex systems, such as quasicrystals, see Section 4.5 and Chapter 5 based
on [18, 16, 31].
A particular focus will be on symbolic dynamical systems defined as follows.
Example 3.3 (Symbolic dynamical systems). Let G be a discrete and countable group and
A be a finite set (called alphabet) equipped with the discrete topology. Then AG := {ω :
G →A} equipped with the product topology is a compact metrizable space. A particular
metric suitable for the spectral applications is defined later in Section 3.2.2. The group G
acts via the shift on AG, namely,
τ : G × AG →AG,
τ(g, ω) := ω ◦g−1,
where ◦denotes the composition of maps. Thus, (AG, G, τ) is a topological dynamical
system. Elements of S for this dynamical system are also called subshift.
If, for instance, G = Zd, then symbolic dynamical systems are used to model solids in
which the letters a ∈A represent different atomic species positioned on the lattice Zd. In
more general settings, the atoms are not restricted to lattice positions. Instead, solids are
modeled by point sets in the ambient space (e.g., G = Rd), where each point is assigned
a label (below called a weight) representing its species.
These point sets are not arbitrary: two atoms cannot be arbitrarily close (the set must
be uniformly discrete), and there should not be arbitrarily large gaps (the set must be
relatively dense). Sets satisfying both conditions are called (weighted) Delone sets, which
are introduced and studied in the following subsection.
We equip the space of weighted Delone sets with a specific metric making it a compact
metric space and induces the various topologies commonly used in the literature. The
ambient group (or any subgroup) acts Lipschitz continuously (Proposition 3.16) on this
space of weighted Delone sets and so defines a suitable class of dynamical systems. More-
over, we show that this framework also encompasses symbolic dynamical systems, thereby
providing a unified perspective.
Before proceeding, we introduce some basic terminology from dynamical systems that
will be used throughout this work. We are particularly interested in both periodic and
non-periodic elements of the dynamical system (Z, G, τ). To formalize this distinction,
we use the concept of the stabilizer Stab(x) of an element x ∈Z, defined by
Stab(x) := {g ∈G : gx = x} .
We note that the stabilizer is always a subgroup of G.
Definition 3.4. Let (Z, G, τ) be a dynamical system. We call x ∈Z
• non-periodic if its stabilizer Stab(x) is trivial, i.e. Stab(x) = {e}.
• periodic if there is discrete subgroup Γ ⊆Stab(x) and a relative compact V ⊆G
such that G := F
γ∈Γ V γ.
If g ∈Stab(x) \ {e}, then we say that x has period g.

Another central concept for this work is that of a dynamical system which cannot be
decomposed (topologically) into a smaller dynamical subsystem:
Definition 3.5. A dynamical system (Z, G, τ) is called minimal if every orbit in Z is
dense; that is, for all x ∈Z, the orbit Orb(x) is dense in Z.
Note that (Z, G, τ) is minimal if and only if S = {Z}, namely, it does not contain any
other dynamical subsystem than itself. In this work, we are mainly interested in a large,
unifying dynamical system (Z, G, τ) which is not minimal, and we study approximations
of minimal dynamical subsystems in the space (S , δH).
3.1
Semicontinuity of the measures
Let (Z, G, τ) be a topological dynamical system and denote by B(Z) the Borel σ-algebra
of Z. A measure µ : B(Z) →[0, ∞) is called a probability measure if µ(Z) = 1. The set of
all probability measures on Z is denoted by M1(Z). Furthermore, a measure µ ∈M1(Z)
is called G-invariant if
µ(g−1A) = µ(A)
for all A ∈B(Z), g ∈G.
If the group G is amenable, then there exists a G-invariant probability measure, see e.g.
[194, Chap. 1]. We postpone the definition of amenability to Appendix 8.3 when we need
it specifically. Denote the set of all G-invariant probability measures by
M1(Z, G) :=

µ ∈M1(Z) : µ is G-invariant
	
.


## Page 23


3.1. SEMICONTINUITY OF THE MEASURES
15
By the Banach-Alaoglu theorem, M1(Z, G) is compact and metrizable in the weak∗-
topology induced from the duality with C(Z), the space of continuous functions on Z.
A measure µ ∈M1(Z, G) is called ergodic if every G-invariant subset A ⊆Z satisfies
µ(A) ∈{0, 1}.
That is, the measure space (Z, B(Z), µ) cannot be decomposed into
smaller (up to µ-null sets) G-invariant measurable subsystems. In this sense, ergodicity
is the measure-theoretic analog of (topological) minimality.
Note that M1(Z, G) is a convex set. In particular, the convex combination of two distinct
ergodic measures on (Z, G, τ) is again a G-invariant measure, but not ergodic in general.
Using the Krein-Milman theorem (see e.g. [213, Thm. 3.15]), one can show that M1(Z, G)
is the closed convex hull of its extreme points, which are precisely the ergodic measures
on (Z, G, τ).
In particular, if there exists only one G-invariant probability measure µ on (Z, G, τ), then
this measure is automatically ergodic explaining the following terminology.
Definition 3.6. We call a dynamical system (Z, G, τ) uniquely ergodic if there exists
exactly one G-invariant probability measure on Z.
The support of a measure µ ∈M1(Z, G) is defined as
supp(µ) := {x ∈Z : µ(f) > 0 for all f ∈C(Z) with f ≥0 and f(x) > 0} ,

a construction also referred to as the canonical extension. Via this embedding, M1(Y, G)
can be viewed as a compact subset of M1(Z, G) with respect to the weak∗-topology [242,

Let T be a metric space and f : T →K(X). We call f upper semi-continuous (respectively
lower semi-continuous) at t0 ∈T, if for all sequences (tn)n∈N ⊆T converging to t0, we
have lim supn→∞f(tn) ⊆f(t0) (respectively f(t0) ⊆lim infn→∞f(tn)).
We use these considerations for the compact space X = M1(Z, G) equipped with the
weak∗-topology.
We do not choose here a particular metric but note that since Z is
compact, the weak∗-topology on M1(Z, G) is compact, second countable and Hausdorff.
Thus, this space is metrizable. We are in particular interested in the compact, convex
subsetes M1(Y, G) of M1(Z, G) for Y ∈S . We obtain the following semi-continuity
results for the map MS proven in the joint work [40, Thm. I].

Since the presentation here deviates from the one in [40, Thm. I], let us shortly explain

Proof. First note that the set lim supn→∞M1(Yn, G) is clearly compact as an intersection

measures µ ∈M1(Z, G) that are limit points of arbitrary sequences µk ∈M1(Yk, G), k ∈
N. It is worth emphasizing that (µk)k∈N ⊆M1(Z, G) is not necessarily convergent in the
weak∗-topology. However, since M1(Z, G) is compact, it admits a limit point. That this
limit point defines an invariant measure in M1(Y, G) is proven [40, Thm. I]. This relies
on the principle that the support of the measure must be contained in the limiting set Y
if limn→∞Yn = Y .

see also eq. (3.2). Specifically, we have to show that every sequence of measures µn ∈

variant measure on the dynamical system (Y, G, τ). Assume towards contradiction that
this is not the case. Then there is a subsequence µnk ∈M1(Ynk, G) that do not converge
to µ. By compactness of M1(Z, G), there is no loss of generality in assuming that the
limit limk→∞µnk =: ν ∈M1(Z, G) exists. Then [40, Lem. 2.1] implies that the support
supp(ν) is contained in Y since limn→∞Yn = Y . Hence, ν ∈M1(Y, G) and since Y is
uniquely ergodic, we conclude ν = µ, a contradiction.
In the upcoming sections, we discuss certain classes of dynamical systems admitting suit-
able (periodic) approximations. The results from this section then imply convergence –
or, in some cases, semi-continuity – of the corresponding invariant measures.
This has further implications for the convergence of two central objects: the autocorre-
lation measure and the density of states of an associated operator. We briefly outline
their relevance in the literature and refer to the joint work [40] for precise definitions and
formal statements concerning the continuity properties of these quantities.
• The autocorrelation measure was developed to provide a rigorous mathematical
framework for diffraction experiments. The discovery of quasicrystals by Dan Shecht-
man [219], for example, was based on a diffraction experiment and relied on the
crystallographic restriction theorem; see e.g. [11, Sec. 3.1].
These solids are modeled by point sets in space that represent the positions of atomic
nuclei. It is assumed that no two points can be arbitrarily close to each other, and
that there are no arbitrarily large holes in the material – a condition formalized by
the notion of Delone sets. By distinguishing different atomic species using colors
(or letters), one obtains weighted Delone sets, which are formally introduced in
Section 3.2.
It has turned out that diffractive properties of Delone sets are reflected in the as-
sociated dynamical systems; see, for instance, the non-exhaustive list of references


## Page 27


3.2. THE SPACE OF WEIGHTED DELONE SETS
19
[106, 217, 160, 13, 14, 120, 172, 204, 11] and references therein. More recently, a
non-commutative spherical diffraction theory has been developed for so-called regu-
lar model sets [58, 59, 60], a special class of cut-and-project sets.
Assuming the conditions of Corollary 3.9, the autocorrelation measure converges
in the weak-∗topology whenever the underlying dynamical systems converge to a
uniquely ergodic dynamical system, see [40, Thm. V.A] for details.
• A similar convergence result holds for the density of states measure (DOS) associ-
ated with a random operator defined over dynamical systems. A particular instance
of such operators are the dynamically-defined operators introduced in Section 4.1,
considered together with an invariant measure on the corresponding dynamical sys-
tem.
One is typically interested in the integrated density of states (IDS), which is the
cumulative distribution function of the DOS. In quantum mechanical models, it
quantifies the number of energy levels per unit volume lying below a given energy
threshold, see also a discussion in Chapter 4 and its relation to the Gap Labeling
theorem.
For suitable uniquely ergodic systems, the IDS can be approximated via finite-volume
analogs using an ergodic theorem; see e.g. [45, 148, 169, 170, 171, 166, 165, 149, 173,
168, 201, 202, 90] and references therein. In [40], the Pastur-Shubin trace formula
[193, 221] provides the appropriate framework: it expresses the IDS as an integral
over the dynamical system.
Assuming the conditions of Corollary 3.9, the DOS measure converges in the weak-∗
topology whenever the underlying dynamical systems converge to a uniquely ergodic
dynamical system, see [40, Thm. V.B] for details.
We also addressed in this work [40] if the hulls of regular model sets can be approximated
by changing the underlying lattice.
We obtain positive results by approximating the
window function via continuous functions with compact support leading naturally to the
notion of weighted Delone sets, studied in the upcoming section. In [19], approximations
for symbolic dynamical systems are discussed; see a discussion in Section 3.6. Note that
all of these examples define dynamical systems that are uniquely ergodic.
We note that the integrated density of states also plays a central role in Chapter 5, where
the joint works [16, 18] are discussed solving the dry ten Martini problem for Sturmian
dynamical systems.
3.2
The space of weighted Delone sets
In order to analyze the convergence of dynamical systems arising in the context of ape-
riodic order within a unified framework, we introduce and study weighted Delone sets in
this section.
Some of the results presented here are new1 in the literature, although they have been
considered as folklore.
The specific definition of the metric on the space of weighted
Delone sets is inspired by the construction of Solomyak [231, 232] (in the setting of tilings
with a fixed collection of prototiles) and the recent joint work [35], which studies weighted
Delone sets as a topological space (see Section 3.3).
1According to the best knowledge of the author.

CHAPTER 3. THE DYNAMICAL AND ERGODIC THEORETICAL PERSPECTIVE
We consider weighted Delone sets in a locally compact, second countable, Hausdorff group
G, define a suitable metric on this space, and characterize the convergence of their asso-
ciated hulls through the convergence of local patterns.
For the sake of readability, the technical details and proofs are deferred to Appendix 8.1.
We also refer the reader to [2, 10, 169, 185, 14, 13, 167, 186, 234, 135, 209, 12, 61] for
earlier work on weighted Delone sets.
Definition 3.11 (Delone sets). Let r0, R0 > 0. A subset D ⊆G is called
• (r0-)uniformly discrete, if ♯B(x, r0) ∩D ≤1 for all x ∈G;
• (R0-)relatively dense, if G = S
x∈D B(R0)(x).
If D is r0-uniformly discrete and R0-relatively dense, then D is called an (r0, R0)-Delone
set. The set of all (r0, R0)-Delone sets is denoted by Del(r0, R0).
The group G acts on Del(r0, R0) via left multiplication, namely
gD := {gx : x ∈D} ,
g ∈G, D ∈Del(r0, R0).
In order to include also symbolic dynamics within the theory developed here, we also
define weighted Delone sets.
Definition 3.12 (Weight). Let σ ≥1 and let P ⊆G be an r0-uniformly discrete set. A
map α : P →[σ−1, σ] is called a (σ-)weight of P.
A tuple (D, α) is called a (σ-)weighted Delone set if D ∈Del(r0, R0) and α is a σ-weight
of D. The set of all σ-weighted Delone sets is denoted by
Del(r0, R0, σ) := {(D, α) : (D, α) is a σ-weighted Delone set} .
The group G naturally acts on Del(r0, R0, σ) via
τ : G × Del(r0, R0, σ) →Del(r0, R0, σ),

7→g · (D, α) := (gD, α ◦g−1).
While it is elementary to verify that this defines a group action, we still need to specify a
topology on Del(r0, R0, σ) in order to determine whether this action is continuous. To this
end, we introduce a specific metric on Del(r0, R0, σ) and compare its induced topology
with those considered in special cases in the literature.
Here the assumption σ ≥1
(namely σ−1 > 0 allows us to work with the associated weighted dirac combs. Before
doing so, let us briefly discuss two main examples treated in this work.
Example 3.13 (Delone dynamical systems). If σ = 1, then every weight is trivial (con-
stant map with range {1}). Thus, we identify Del(r0, R0) with Del(r0, R0, σ) if σ = 1 via
the bijective map D 7→(D, 1). Note that this map is compatible with the group action on
Del(r0, R0) since g.(D, 1) = (gD, 1) for D ∈Del(r0, R0).
Example 3.14 (Symbolic dynamical systems). Let Γ ⊆G be a discrete cocompact sub-
group (i.e. G/Γ is compact) and A a finite set. Then Γ ∈Del(r0, R0) for a suitable choice
of r0, R0 > 0. The elements of the configuration space AΓ := {ω : Γ →A} can be repre-
sented as weighted Delone sets. In order to do so, we assume without loss of generality
(identify letters with positive real numbers) that A ⊆[σ−1, σ] for some σ > 1. Then the
map
AΓ →Del(r0, R0, σ),
ω 7→(Γ, ω)
is injective and provides an embedding of AΓ into Del(r0, R0, σ). The group Γ naturally
acts on AΓ via gω := ω ◦g−1 for g ∈Γ and ω ∈AΓ, see Example 3.3 where symbolic


## Page 29


3.2. THE SPACE OF WEIGHTED DELONE SETS
21
dynamical systems are defined. Then the previously defined embedding is compatible with
the Γ-action.
After defining the metric structure on Del(r0, R0, σ), we show in Section 3.2.2 that this
embedding is homeomorphic (even bi-Lipschitz). Therefore symbolic dynamical systems
are represented by weighted Delone sets.
In the literature also compact alphabets are
studied. Note that the previous construction works also for alphabets being compact subsets
embedded into [σ−1, σ].
Both examples are naturally endowed with a metrizable topology.
Specifically, AΓ is
equipped with the product topology, which defines a compact metrizable space. In this
topology, two configurations in AΓ are close to each other if they coincide on a large ball
around the identity element e ∈G.
Since each Delone set is a closed subset of G, the space Del(r0, R0) can be equipped with
the Chabauty-Fell topology [76, 109], making Del(r0, R0) a compact, metrizable space;
see e.g. [61, Sec. 3]. Roughly speaking, two Delone sets are close to each other if they
coincide on a large ball around the identity element e ∈G, up to a small displacement.
We emphasize that the Chabauty-Fell topology on the space of Delone sets is generally
not induced by the Hausdorff metric on subsets of G, since Delone sets are merely closed
subsets of G. In particular, the Hausdorff metric is defined on K(G), the space of compact
subsets of G, while Delone sets are typically closed and discrete, but not compact.
For Delone sets, various similar notions of convergence and topologies have been proposed,
particularly for certain subclasses such as sets of finite local complexity or repetitive sets;
see, e.g., [106, 231, 232].
By classical metrization theorems, the Chabauty-Fell topology on Del(r0, R0) is metriz-
able, as it is compact, second countable, and Hausdorff. Consequently, one can find several
constructions of explicit metrics compatible with this topology, for instance in the setting
of Delone sets of finite local complexity [56] and tiling spaces [211, 212].
Following [13, 34, 61], the weighted Delone sets can be endowed with the weak∗-topology
by identifying weighted Delone sets with their associated weighted dirac comb - defining
a Radon measure on the Borel σ-algebra of G. We follow a similar approach here but also
defining a metric on the set of r0-uniformly discrete weighted sets
U(r0, σ) := {(P, α) : P is r0-uniformly discrete and α is a σ-weight of P} .
Clearly Del(r0, R0, σ) ⊆U(r0, σ) holds since we just dropped the assumption of being
R0-relatively dense.
For Π := (P, α) ∈U(r0, σ), the weighted dirac comb on the Borel σ-algebra B(G) of G is
defined by

It is well-known that (U(r0, σ), G, τ) and (Del(r0, R0, σ), G, τ) define a topological dy-

Proposition 3.16. Let G be a lcsc group, r0, R0 > 0 and σ ≥1. Then (U(r0, σ), G, τ)
and (Del(r0, R0, σ), G, τ) are a topological dynamical system. Moreover, the group action

dwD(g.Π, g.Ξ) ≤(dG(e, g) + 1)dwD(Π, Ξ).

Following [26, 30], the convergence of invariant closed subsets of the dynamical system
(Del(r0, R0, σ), G, τ) can be characterized by the convergence of the associated patches.
We provide a unifying framework here for weighted Delone sets.
Let Sσ denote the compact metric space of invariant, closed, and non-empty subsets
of Del(r0, R0, σ), equipped with the Hausdorff metric δH induced by the metric dwD on
Del(r0, R0, σ). We refer to the elements of Sσ as (weighted) Delone dynamical systems.
A canonical way to obtain elements of Sσ is via the hull of a weighted Delone set. For a
weighted Delone set Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ), its hull is defined as
hull(Π) := {g.Π : g ∈G} = Orb(Π) ∈Sσ,
where the closure is taken with respect to the metric dwD.
In the literature, it is common to consider only the transversal associated with a Delone
set; see [169, 170, 48, 29, 40]. For a weighted Delone set Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ), its transversal
is defined as
tran(Π) := {Ξ ∈hull(Π) : µΞ({e}) > 0} = {g−1.Π : g ∈supp(Π)},
where the closure is taken with respect to the metric dwD.
More generally, for a weighted Delone dynamical system Ω∈Sσ, the associated transver-
sal is given by
tran(Ω) := {Ξ ∈Ω: µΞ({e}) > 0} = {g−1.Π : Π ∈Ω, g ∈supp(Π)}.
Since µΞ({e}) > 0 is equivalent to µΞ({e}) ≥σ−1 > 0 for all Ξ ∈Del(r0, R0, σ), every
transversal tran(Ω) is a closed subset of Del(r0, R0, σ) with respect to dwD, and the
collection
S e
σ := {tran(Ω) : Ω∈Sσ}
of all such transversals can be viewed as a subset of K(Del(r0, R0, σ)).
It is straightforward to verify that S e
σ is closed in K(Del(r0, R0, σ)) with respect to the
induced Hausdorff metric δH. In particular, the limit of a sequence of transversals is again
a transversal. Therefore, (S e
σ , δH) is a compact metric space.
Note that a transversal is, in general, not invariant under the action of G; however, if
Ξ ∈tran(Ω) and g ∈supp(Ξ), then g−1.Ξ ∈tran(Ω). To each transversal, one naturally
associates a groupoid, and elements of the associated reduced groupoid C∗-algebra define
the operators of interest in this work; see [169, 170, 29] and Example 6.6.
The (weighted) Delone dynamical system Ω∈Sσ can be reconstructed from its transver-
sal tran(Ω) ⊆Ωas follows.
Lemma 3.17. Let r0, R0 > 0 and σ ≥1. If Ω∈Sσ, then
Ω= B(R0).tran(Ω) :=

g.Π : g ∈B(R0), Π ∈tran(Ω)
	
.
In particular, Ω= G.tran(Ω).
Proof. The inclusion B(R0).tran(Ω) ⊆Ωis obvious.
For the converse, let Ξ ∈Ω⊆
Del(r0, R0, σ). Since supp(Ξ) is R0-relatively dense, there is a g ∈supp(Ξ) ∩B(R0).
Then Π := g−1.Ξ ∈tran(Ω) follows and so Ξ = g.Π ∈B(R0).tran(Ω) is concluded.
With this at hand, we conclude that the convergence of weighted Delone dynamical sys-
tems is characterized by the convergence of the associated transversals. This has been
proven for Delone sets in [40, Thm. 2.A]. The extension to weighted Delone sets is straight-
forward and follows similar lines. We provide in the appendix a different proof for weighted
Delone sets using Lemma 3.17.

Properties of Delone sets (or tilings) are often analyzed by studying the associated patches;
see, e.g., [112, 204, 11]. Since general Delone sets may admit infinitely many patches of a
fixed size, one typically classifies them up to ε-similarity; see [117, 35].
Definition 3.20 (ε-similar patches). For ε > 0, we say two patches Π, Ξ ∈U(r0, σ) are
ε-similar (Π =ε Ξ), if δwD(Π, Ξ) < ε. Two subsets W, W ′ ⊆U(r0, σ) are called ε-similar
(W =ε W ′) if
• for all Λ ∈W, there is a Θ ∈W ′ such that Λ and Θ are ε-similar, and
• for all Θ ∈W ′, there is a Λ ∈W such that Λ and Θ are ε-similar.
We emphasize that the relation of being ε-similar is not an equivalence relation. This
relation is only reflexive and symmetric but not transitive. We next introduce the set of
R-patches associated with a weighted Delone set or weighted Delone dynamical system.

A weighted Delone set Π = (D, α) is called of finite local complexity (FLC) if WR(Π) is
finite for all R > 0. This condition can be characterized by the fact that the difference
set D−1D is locally finite and the weight function α takes only finitely many values; see
[155, 217] in the unweighted case.
While not all Delone sets are of finite local complexity, one can partition any hull or
transversal by neighborhoods of patches of arbitrary fixed size.

implying δwD(Π, Ξ) < eε < c0.
By interchanging the role of T1 and T2, we conclude
δtran ≤eε = δ + ε. Sending ε to zero implies δtran ≤δ.
Next we prove δ ≤δtran. Without loss if generality assume δtran < c0 since δ ≤c0. Let
ε > 0 be such that eε := δtran + ε < c0. Let Π|B(1/eε) ∈W1/eε(T1) be arbitrary. Then
there is a Ξ ∈T2 satisfying dwD(Π, Ξ) < eε implying δwD(Π|B(1/eε), Ξ|B(1/eε)) < eε. Hence,
Π|B(1/eε) =eε Ξ|B(1/eε) ∈W1/eε(T2) follows.
By interchanging the role of T1 and T2, we
conclude δ ≤eε = δtran + ε. Sending ε to zero implies δ ≤δtran.
The previous equality is a practical tool to construct periodic approximations and estimate
the rate of convergence [26, 30, 19] (see also Section 3.6) or identify possible spectral
defects [31] (Section 4.5). The estimates on the rate of convergence for the dynamical
systems yields then corresponding estimates for the rate of convergence of the spectrum
[28, 41] (Section 4.1). If one is only interested in convergence of Delone dynamical systems,
we can summarize the previous results in the following corollary.
Corollary 3.25. Let r0, R0 > 0 and σ ≥1. Then the following are equivalent for a
sequence of weighted Delone dynamical systems Ω∈Sσ, n ∈N, and Ω∈Sσ.
(i) lim

We finish the section by briefly discussing the implications for the particular case of Delone
sets (without a weight) and symbolic dynamical systems of the previous results.
3.2.1
Delone dynamical systems
Following Example 3.13, we identify Del(r0, R0) with Del(r0, R0, σ) by setting σ = 1,
corresponding to trivial weights. In this case, the topology on Del(r0, R0) = Del(r0, R0, σ)
induced by dwD coincides with the Chabauty-Fell topology; see, e.g., [13, Lem. 2], [186,
Sec. 2.1] and [61, Prop. 3.2]. As for weighted Delone sets, the hull of a Delone set is
defined as the orbit closure
hull(D) := {g.D : g ∈G}.

3.2. THE SPACE OF WEIGHTED DELONE SETS

For the sake of simplicity, we focus on Delone sets of finite local complexity (FLC),
meaning that the set of associated R-patches
WR(D) :=

g−1D ∩B(R) : g ∈D
	
is finite for each R > 0. This is equivalent to the condition that the difference set D−1D
is locally finite, or, equivalently, that D−1D is closed and discrete. These patches describe
the hull (under the FLC assumption) via
hull(D) = {P ∈Del(r0, R0) : WR(P) ⊆WR(D) for all R > 0} .
Let L ⊆G be a discrete and closed set.
Then L is locally finite, and every Delone
set D ∈Del(r0, R0) with D−1D ⊆L is automatically of finite local complexity. The
corresponding space
Del(r0, R0, L) :=

D ∈Del(r0, R0) : D−1D ⊆L
	
is a closed subset of Del(r0, R0). This space is also endowed with the so-called local rubber
topology [13], which coincides with the previously described topology on Del(r0, R0).
We now reformulate Corollary 3.25 when restricting to Del(r0, R0, L).
Proposition 3.26. Let r0, R0 > 0 and L ⊆G be a discrete and closed set. Then the
following are equivalent for Dn, D ∈Del(r0, R0, L), n ∈N.
(i) limn→∞hull(Dn) = hull(D) in Sσ.
(ii) For all R > 0, there exists an n0 ∈N such that WR(Dn) = WR(D) for all n ≥n0.
Proof. Since D−1D ⊆L, each R-patch of D ∈Del(r0, R0, L) is a subset of L∩B(R). This
intersection is finite because L is discrete and closed. Consequently, there are only finitely
many subsets of L∩B(R), and thus only finitely many possible R-patches, uniformly for all
D ∈Del(r0, R0, L). Hence, the statement follows immediately from Proposition 3.24.

3.3
Repetitivity of weighted Delone sets
We address the question of how dynamical properties of the hull of a Delone set are
reflected in the structure of the underlying Delone set.
In this section, we focus on
minimality and unique ergodicity of Delone dynamical systems, following the framework
developed in [35].
Minimality of a Delone dynamical system is characterized by repetitivity of the underlying
Delone set; see, e.g., [156, 244, 117, 35]. Roughly speaking, this means that every finite
patch occurs in any sufficiently large compact region, uniformly over the space. If the
required region size depends nicely on the patch size (e.g., linearly), then the corresponding
dynamical system is even uniquely ergodic. More precisely, linearly repetitive (or linearly
recurrent) configurations define uniquely ergodic systems – a result due to Durand [105]
in the symbolic setting for G = Z and Lagarias and Pleasants [156] for Delone sets in Rd.
In fact, these systems are strictly ergodic, i.e., both minimal and uniquely ergodic.
Various extensions of this principle have been developed in different directions; see [95,
163, 164, 53, 165, 200, 54, 117, 202]. The present work [35] generalizes these results to
the non-Abelian setting and to weighted Delone sets.
Following an approach by Damanik and Lenz [95], Delone sets that are linearly repetitive
satisfy uniform subadditive convergence theorems. Such ergodic theorems have a wide
range of applications, including the existence of Banach densities [103] and of the inte-
grated density of states (IDS) [171, 202]. The IDS will play a fundamental role in the
developments described in Chapter 5.
Classically, the ergodic averages are taken over balls in the group. In the setting of general
groups, these are replaced by so-called (strong) Følner sequences. To handle this in our
recent work [35], we employed the Ornstein-Weiss machinery [189] based on ε-quasi tilings,
further developed in [202].
In the context of aperiodic order, two prominent classes of Delone sets are of central
interest: cut-and-project sets and substitution systems [204, 112, 11]. Recently, there
has been considerable interest in extending related results beyond the Abelian case. For
cut-and-project sets, see e.g. [59, 60, 58, 62]; for substitution systems, see [34, 19].
We emphasize that, thanks to the results in [34] (see also Section 3.4), there exists a rich
class of linearly repetitive systems far beyond the Abelian setting.
In the work [35], the notion of linear repetitivity has been extended in several directions:
• to weighted Delone sets, thereby covering both classical Delone sets and symbolic
dynamical systems as discussed in the previous section;


## Page 38


30
CHAPTER 3. THE DYNAMICAL AND ERGODIC THEORETICAL PERSPECTIVE
• to amenable, unimodular lcsc groups G: if the group G is not of exact polynomial
growth, it is unclear whether balls still form strong Følner sequences. This motivates
a metric-free formulation, referred to as tempered repetitivity of a (weighted) Delone
set with respect to a given strong Følner sequence;
• to weighted Delone sets without finite local complexity, leading to the notion of
almost tempered repetitivity, inspired by ideas from Frettl¨oh and Richard [117].
For the sake of presentation, we restrict ourselves here to the case where the group has
exact polynomial growth, and refer the reader to [35] for a more detailed discussion.
Let G be an lcsc group with an adapted metric dG (see Chapter 3 for the definition) and
left-invariant Haar measure mG on the Borel σ-algebra of G [113]. Recall that B(r) :=
B(e, r) denotes the open ball of radius r > 0 around e ∈G.
Definition 3.28. The group G is called of exact polynomial growth if there are constants
C > 0 and κ ≥0 such that
lim

= 1.

To make this clear consider first the following example.

Definition 3.30 (S-patch). For a relatively compact set S ⊆G, a tuple (Π|S, S) is called
an S-patch of Π ∈U(r0, σ).
Note that we do this particular distinction only in this section.
Recall the notion of

To capture a quantitative version of approximate pattern recurrence, we introduce the
notion of almost repetitivity. Roughly speaking, this means that every R-patch ε-occurs
within a patch of radius ϱ(ε, R) ≥1.
Definition 3.31. A function ϱ : (0, 1) × [1, ∞) →[1, ∞) is called an almost repetitivity
function for Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ) if for every ε > 0 and all R ≥1, the following holds: For
all g, h ∈G, the patch
 Π|gB(R), gB(R)

 Π|hB(ϱ(ε,R)), hB(ϱ(ε, R))

Whenever such a function exists for Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ), we call Π almost repetitive.
Following [35, Prop. 2.4], the dynamical system hull(Π) is minimal if and only if Π ∈
Del(r0, R0, σ) is almost repetitive. The proof follows the lines of [117], where the result
is established for (unweighted) Delone sets.
We are in particular interested in the following class of almost repetitive weighted Delone
sets.


## Page 39


3.4. SUBSTITUTION SYSTEMS: GOING BEYOND THE ABELIAN WORLD
31
Definition 3.32. Let dG be an adapted metric on the group G. A weighted Delone set
Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ) is called almost linearly repetitive with respect to dG if there is a
function cΠ : (0, ∞) →(0, ∞) such that ϱ : (0, 1) × [1, ∞) →[1, ∞) defined by
ϱ(ε, R) = cΠ(ε)R,
(ε, R) ∈(0, 1) × [1, ∞),
is an almost repetivity function of Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ).
A weighted Delone set Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ) is called linearly repetitive (LR) with respect to
dG if it is almost linearly repetitive with respect to dG and the constant cΠ > 0 is uniformly
bounded in ε > 0.
With these notions at hand, we proved the following result.
Theorem 3.33 ([35]). Let G be a locally compact second countable Hausdorff group with
exact polynomial growth with respect to an adapted metric dG. If Π ∈Del(r0, R0, σ) is
almost linearly repetitive, then hull(Π) is minimal and uniquely ergodic.
Proof. This is proven in [35, Thm 1.2].
A metric-free version of this result – formulated in terms of tempered repetitivity with
respect to a strong Følner sequence – is provided in [35, Thm. 1.3].
In the following we construct a class of linearly repetitive weighted Delone sets.
3.4
Substitution systems: Going beyond the Abelian world
At first glance, it is not obvious whether linearly repetitive (LR) Delone sets exist at
all beyond the Abelian setting.
In Zd or Rd, so-called primitive substitution systems
give rise to LR Delone sets; see [230, 98].
In the joint work [34], our main aim was
to construct LR Delone sets for homogeneous Lie groups. Therefore we developed the
notion of (primitive) substitutions for homogeneous Lie groups G and lattices Γ ⊆G.
Since substitutions inherently involve enlarging and subdividing patterns, a compatible
dilation structure is required. To this end, nilpotent Lie groups equipped with a dilation
structure are considered.
It turned out that this framework produces a rich class of aperiodic, linearly repetitive
Delone sets. Combined with the results from [35], presented in Section 3.3 (Theorem 3.33),
the associated dynamical systems are uniquely ergodic. Among other consequences, this
leads to the continuity of certain measure-theoretic quantities – such as the integrated
density of states and the autocorrelation – when combined with the stability results in
[40] (see also Section 3.1).
A typical example of a homogeneous Lie group is the Heisenberg group H3(R). As a set,
this group coincides with R3, which might suggest that one could transfer LR sets from
R3 to H3(R). However, we emphasize that the notion of LR depends on shifting a ball
around within the group. In the non-Abelian setting – such as H3(R) – balls become
distorted (as subsets of R3) when translated (see Figure 3.11 in Section 3.5), and thus
naive extensions of LR from the Euclidean setting have failed so far.
We note that there exist various extensions of substitution systems in the Abelian setting,
including, for instance: random substitutions [214], substitutions over a compact alphabet
[181], and substitutions with multiple inflation factors [227], see also the review [180]
for more references. Beyond the Abelian context, substitution systems have also been
explored in non-Euclidean settings such as the hyperbolic plane [43] and on trees [22].
We outline here the approach of (symbolic) substitution systems developed in [34] and
provide few examples.

Since K is a fundamental domain of 2Z2 ⊆Z2, this construction is well-defined: there are

By a compactness argument, one concludes that the iteration on a single letter (as sketched
in Figure 3.3) has a limit point ω0 ∈AZ2 satisfying S2(ω0) = ω0, and composed only of
legal patches (defined below). Such a configuration ω0 is also called fixed point of the
substitution.
Since the substitution is primitive (see below), it follows that the orbit
closure Ωtable := Orb(ω0) is independent of the chosen limit point and defines a strictly
ergodic dynamical system (Ωtable, Z2, τ).
This construction naturally extends to Zd, but at first glance becomes more intricate
when considering non-Abelian groups, as sets deform their geometry under translation.

To overcome this difficulty, we formalized the concept in [34] to the setting of homogeneous
Lie groups G. A substitution system in this context is described using certain geometric
data, referred to as a dilation datum D = (G, dG, (Dλ)λ>0, Γ, V ), consisting of:
(D1) a 1-connected, locally compact, second-countable, Hausdorff group G, equipped with
an adapted metric dG;

family, such that

V ⊆G for Γ (i.e. G = ΓV = F

the interior of V .
Alongside the dilation datum, we have combinatorial data, referred to as a substitution

(S1) a finite set A, referred to as the alphabet;
(S2) a stretch factor λ0 > 1, associated with the dilation datum D (see below for details);
(S3) a substitution rule S0 : A →ADλ0(V )∩Γ.

here is weaker and accommodates all block substitutions in this framework; see [19,
Prop. 3.1] briefly discussed in Section 3.5. An updated version of [34] will reflect
this adjustment in the definition of “sufficiently large” accordingly. For convenience,
the adjustments and proofs are provided in the Appendix 8.2.
We prove in [34] that under this condition the sets V (n) grow comparably to metric balls
in G, see also Theorem 8.6 in the Appendix 8.2. Also we provide sufficient criteria in
Lemma 8.4, which we apply in the following for some examples.

in the notion of sufficiently large is necessary to include this example. Specifically, V (n)
only contains a shifted ball with radius comparable to λn

This example illustrates how the classical table tiling substitution naturally fits into the

Proof. This is proven in [34, Prop. 2.7, Prop. 5.6, Prop. 5.12, Lem. 5.16].
Definition 3.38 (Substitution map). Let S = (A, λ0, S0) be a substitution datum over
the dilation datum D = (G, dG, (Dλ)λ>0, Γ, V ). The unique map S : Pat →Pat defined
in Proposition 3.37, which satisfies the equivariance and restriction conditions, is called
the substitution map associated with D and S.
Proposition 3.37 (b) justifies that the recursively defined sets V (n, M) from eq. (3.7)
indeed describe the support of the n-fold substituted patch Sn(P), where P : M →A.
Moreover, Proposition 3.37 (c) provides an explicit expression for how the substitution
map acts pointwise.
We now proceed to define the associated dynamical system Ω(S) ⊆AΓ for a substitution
map S associated with D and S.
There are various approaches in the literature to
construct such systems, either via fixed points or via the set of legal patches; see for
instance [204, 11].
In Section 3.3, we already defined when a patch ε-occurs in another one. Since we study
here symbolic dynamical systems over a finite alphabet, we can drop the ε-dependence.
Definition 3.39 (Subpatch relation). A patch P : M →A ∈Pat is a subpatch of (or
occurs in patch) Q : F →A ∈Pat, if there exists γ ∈Γ such that γM ⊆F and
Q(γη) = P(η)
for all η ∈M.
In this case, we write P ≺Q.

Proof. This is proven in [34, Thm. 1.2].
We now address the question under which conditions the subshift Ω(S) is minimal and
uniquely ergodic. The key tool is the notion of linear repetitivity.
Definition 3.42 (Primitive substitution). Let S be a substitution map associated with D
and S = (A, λ0, S0). We call S primitive if there exists L ∈N such that for all a, b ∈A,
one has a ≺SL(b).
It is straightforward to check that the table tiling substitution is primitive with L = 2:
each letter of the alphabet occurs in the second substitution iterate S2(a) for any a ∈A,
as illustrated in Figure 3.5.

Theorem 3.43 ([34]). Let S be a substitution map associated with D and S = (A, λ0, S0).
If S is primitive, then the associated substitution subshift Ω(S) is strictly ergodic, i.e.,
minimal and uniquely ergodic. Moreover, every element in Ω(S) is linearly repetitive.
Proof. The result is proven in [34, Thm. 1.2].
For context, we note that primitivity
ensures minimality and linear repetitivity of all configurations in Ω(S); see [34, Thm. 7.4].
Moreover, the lattice Γ has exact polynomial growth (see Section 3.3 for a definition) by
[34, Sec. 3.4]. Thus, Γ is amenable by [35, Prop. 4.4]. Combining these properties with
the results of [35], in particular Theorem 3.33, yields unique ergodicity of Ω(S).
To further analyze the dynamical structure, we investigated under which conditions el-
ements in the subshift are non-periodic. We call a subshift Ω⊆AΓ weakly aperiodic
if it contains at least one non-periodic element; see Definition 3.4. If all elements are
non-periodic, we call Ωstrongly aperiodic. In the Abelian case, weak aperiodicity and
minimality already imply strong aperiodicity. This implication does not directly carry
over in the non-Abelian setting and requires separate analysis; see a discussion in [34,
Sec. 8.2].
One classical strategy to establish aperiodicity in symbolic substitutions in Zd involves the
existence of proximal pairs [11, 23]. In geometric settings, injectivity of the substitution
often suffices to construct aperiodic configurations [229, 230, 1]. Inspired by the latter,
we introduce a notion of non-periodicity for general substitution systems:
Definition 3.44 (Non-periodic substitution). Let S be a substitution map associated with
D and S = (A, λ0, S0). Then S is called non-periodic if S0 is injective and
 γ−1S(a)
 
γ−1D(V )∩D(V ) ̸= S(b)

γ−1D(V )∩D(V )
for all γ ∈D(V ) ∩Γ \ {e} and a, b ∈A.


## Page 46


38
CHAPTER 3. THE DYNAMICAL AND ERGODIC THEORETICAL PERSPECTIVE
This notion of non-periodic substitution has not been introduced in the Abelian setting,
as alternative methods are typically used to establish aperiodicity.
Theorem 3.45 ([34]). Let S be a non-periodic substitution. Then the associated subshift
Ω(S) is weakly aperiodic. In particular, every fixed point ω ∈Ω(S) is non-periodic.
We emphasize that the non-periodicity of the substitution rule S guarantees that all of
its fixed points are non-periodic.
Whether non-periodicity of S also implies strong aperiodicity of Ω(S) remains an open
and subtle question. Partial results have been obtained for 2-step nilpotent Lie groups;
see [34, Thm. 1.4]. This includes, in particular, the discrete Heisenberg group, discussed
in Example 3.49 below.
In contrast to the Abelian case – where minimality combined with weak aperiodicity
implies strong aperiodicity – there is currently no general argument that establishes this
implication in the non-Abelian setting. We suspect, however, that the partial result for
2-step nilpotent Lie groups may extend more broadly – possibly even to all homogeneous
Lie groups – by an inductive argument along the lines of [34].
3.5
A zoo of examples
Building on the substitution framework developed in [34] (as presented in the previous
section), it is natural to ask under which conditions such substitutions exist and whether
classical examples fall within this setting. We briefly address these questions here and
refer the reader to the more detailed discussion in [34]. In addition, we present a selection
of concrete examples.
In order to construct such substitutions explicitly, the original work [34] introduces a
structural splitting condition with respect to an Abelian component of the group. This
leads to the notion of RAHOGRASP groups (rationally homogeneous Lie groups with
rational spectrum) and the concept of a good substitution rule.
Theorem 3.46 ([34]). For every RAHOGRASP group G of dimension at least two and
every finite alphabet A with #A ≥2, there exists a dilation datum D over G and a
primitive and non-periodic substitution datum S over D with alphabet A.
The proof given in [34, Thm. 1.1] is constructive and uses the notion of a good substitution
rule as defined in [34, Sec. 6.2]. This construction still leaves considerable flexibility in the
choice of the actual substitution, but defines a primitive and non-periodic substitution.
In Example 3.49, we provide an example of a good substitution rule for the Heisenberg
group.
As a direct consequence of Theorem 3.46, we conclude in [34, Cor. 1.5] that every RA-
HOGRASP group G admits a linearly repetitive and non-periodic Delone set.
Let us return to the symbolic dynamical systems. A natural question is whether this set-
ting includes classically studied examples such as block substitutions. A block substitution
on AZd with finite alphabet A is defined via a vector m = (m1, . . . , md) ∈Nd with mj > 1
for all 1 ≤j ≤d, and a substitution rule S0 : A →AK(m), where
K(m) := Zd ∩

is the support of the substitution rule. As in the case of the table tiling discussed previ-
ously, the rule extends uniquely to a substitution map S : Pat →Pat; see [229, 230, 114,
11].

We emphasize that the entries mj in a block substitution may differ, allowing for non-

3.6

In Section 3.2, we discussed conditions under which the hulls of weighted Delone dynam-
ical systems converge. As a direct application, we showed for symbolic systems (Proposi-
tion 3.27) that two subshifts are close to each other if they share the same patches up to
a fixed size.

measure theoretic quantities (Section 3.1) and we see further spectral applications in
Section 4.1. Motivated by various approaches in one dimension, and based on preliminary
ideas in [26], we raised the following question in [19]:

A central idea is to associate a finite graph to a substitution and to characterize conver-

relation ≺(Definition 3.39) and the notion of legal patches W(S) associated with a sub-

A tuple G = (V, E) is called a directed graph if V is a countable set and E ⊆V × V. We
emphasize that elements of E are ordered pairs, so in general (P, Q) ̸= (Q, P). The set V
is referred to as the vertex set, and E as the edge set. An element (P, Q) ∈E is interpreted
as a directed edge from P to Q. A (directed) path in a directed graph is a chain of vertices
(P0, . . . , Pn) such that (Pi, Pi+1) ∈E for all i ∈{0, . . . , n −1}.
Definition 3.51 (Substitution graph). Let S be a substitution map associated with a
dilation datum D = (G, dG, (Dλ)λ>0, Γ, V ) and a substitution datum S = (A, λ0, S0). For

In Figure 3.14, we observe that the non-legal patches P, Q /∈W(S) satisfy P ≺S(Q) and

If either P or Q occurs in a configuration ω0 ∈AΓ, then any iterative Sn(ω0) contains

converge to the table tiling subshift Ωtable = Ω(S). This is one idea to prove Theorem 3.53
using that (P, Q, P) is a closed path in GS(T, 1).
We include here a characterization of the convergence of the spectra while the class of
operators is only introduced below in Section 4.1. So-called dynamically-defined operators
H are associated with the unifying symbolic dynamical system (AΓ, Γ, τ). Here, H =
(Hω)ω∈AΓ is a family of bounded linear operators on a Hilbert space that is strongly
continuous in ω and covariant under the action of Γ; see a detailed discussion in Section 4.1.
Since each Hω is bounded, its spectrum is a compact subset of C, and spectral convergence
is understood in terms of the Hausdorff metric dH induced by the Euclidean metric on C.
Furthermore, a bounded linear operator is called normal if it commutes with its adjoint.


## Page 55


3.6. APPROXIMATION FOR SYMBOLIC SUBSTITUTIONS
47
Theorem 3.53 ([19]). Let S be a substitution map associated with a dilation datum
D = (G, dG, (Dλ)λ>0, Γ, V ) and a substitution datum S = (A, λ0, S0). Assume that S is
primitive and that (T, NT) is a testing tuple. Then the following statements are equivalent:
(i) For every dynamically-defined operator H that is normal, we have
lim
n→∞σ(HSn(ω0)) = σ(Hω),
for all ω ∈Ω(S).
(ii) The iterative subshifts Orb(Sn(ω0)), n ∈N, converge to Ω(S) in the metric space
(S , δA).
(iii) Each directed path in the substitution graph GS(T; NT) that starts in a vertex from
W(ω0) ∩AT does not contain a closed cycle.
Proof. We first observe that σ(Hω) = σ(Hρ) for all ω, ρ ∈Ω(S), which follows from
standard arguments using the minimality of Ω(S), as ensured by the primitivity of S; see
e.g. Corollary 8.12. The equivalence between (i) and (ii) was established in [29], while the
equivalence between (ii) and (iii) is proven in [19, Thm. 2.14].
Theorem 3.53 provides a graph-theoretic criterion to determine the convergence described
in eq. (3.8) respectively of the spectra. This criterion enables the explicit construction
of periodic approximations for the associated operators; see [19, Cor. 2.16]. A sufficient
criteria for the convergence of these subshifts for block substitutions in Zd was existing
before [26]. The new characterization to these graphs for a much larger class of substi-
tutions opens also the door for further analysis such as the study of spectral defects and
dynamical impurities [20]; see a similar discussion in Section 4.5.
Another consequence of Theorem 3.53 is that there exists a primitive substitution in
AZ2 whose associated subshift is not periodically approximable; see [19, Cor. 2.17]. This
stands in contrast to the one-dimensional setting, where all primitive substitutions yield
periodically approximable subshifts; see [30].
Recall from Definition 3.4 the notion of periodic elements in a dynamical system.
A
subshift Ω∈S is called periodically approximable if there exists a sequence of periodic
configurations (ωn)n∈N such that their orbits Orb(ωn) converge to Ωin (S , δA).
In [19, Prop. 2.15], it is shown that if ω0 ∈AΓ is periodic, then S(ω0) is also periodic.
In combination with Theorem 3.53, this implies that Ω(S) is periodically approximable
whenever there exists a periodic configuration ω0 ∈AΓ satisfying one of the equivalent
conditions of the theorem.
To illustrate this, we return to the table tiling substitution. The following result was
originally established in [26, Prop. 7.7.1] using a sufficient condition for convergence, and
is restated in [19, Prop. 1.3]. However, Theorem 3.53 additional gives that single letter
approximations fail to converge for the table tiling substitution:

In a forthcoming preprint we analyze the structure of substitution graphs for block substi-
tutions and their spectral consequences in more detail; see [20]. We observed that already
in dimension two, new phenomena arise. These were initially observed through numerical
investigations of the poor approximation behavior in the table tiling, notably when start-
ing from constant configurations such as ωa from Example 3.52 and Proposition 3.54 (b).
Once a suitable approximation is established, the natural next step is to quantify the
convergence rate. This question will also be central in the upcoming Section 4.1 discussing
the results obtained in [28, 41]. Under the assumption that the subshift associated with a
configuration under iteration of the substitution converges, we prove that the convergence
in (S , δA) is exponentially fast:
