3.7
Periodic approximations: The octagonal or Ammann-Beenker
tiling
We conclude this chapter with a brief discussion of further applications to the particular
geometric example of the octagonal tiling, also known as the Ammann–Beenker tiling.


## Page 57


3.7. THE AMMANN-BEENKER TILING
49
To every Delone set one can associate a tiling, for instance via the Voronoi construction.
Conversely, one can associate to a tiling a point set. Under suitable assumptions, the
dynamical properties are preserved; see e.g. [121, 218, 11] for details.
We refer the reader to [104] for a comprehensive discussion of the octagonal tiling, and
also to [11, Sec. 6.1]. The tiling consists of two prototiles (up to rotation): a square and a
rhombus with opening angle π
4. In some representations, the square is replaced by a pair
of triangles.
If we associate to each vertex (i.e., the point where two edges meet) a point, we obtain a
Delone set of finite local complexity. The space of tilings then inherits a natural transla-
tion action of R2, and the resulting dynamical system can be equipped with a topology
compatible with that of the associated Delone dynamical system.
Figure 3.16: A sketch of a periodic tiling T0 defined in [104]. For convenience, some of the
fundamental cells are shaded in gray to distinguish them.
In [104], the authors constructed a periodic tiling T0 using the aforementioned two tiles,
as sketched in Figure 3.16. For clarity, the boundary of a fundamental cell is marked with
a thick red line. The 1-corona of a tile V refers to the collection of all tiles intersecting
V , i.e., those within one adjacency step.
It can be verified that all 1-corona in the tiling T0 are legal patches in the octagonal tiling,
meaning they appear in the infinite aperiodic tiling. These patches play the role of the
set AT where T was one of the parameters in the testing tuple discussed in Section 3.6.
The octagonal tiling is defined via a substitution rule.
Let S denote the associated
substitution map. Adapting the convergence criteria from [19, Sec. 4.1], one concludes
that the orbits of the periodic tilings Sn(T0) converge to the hull of octagonal tiling.
Hence, the octagonal tiling is periodically approximable.
Remark 3.56. In contrast to the Ammann–Beenker tiling, a Penrose tiling does not
admit periodic approximations using the same tiles. Consider for instance the Penrose
tiling described in [121, Sec. 10.3] and assume there exists a sequence of periodic tilings
converging to it. Then Proposition 3.26 implies the existence of a periodic tiling having the
same 1-coronas as the Penrose tiling. However, by [121, Sec. 10.3.1], no such periodic
tiling exists for this set of tiles. Consequently, periodic approximations of the Penrose
tiling can only be obtained by deforming the underlying tiles.

## Page 59


4
The world of spectral butterflies
In 1976, Douglas Hofstadter [134] numerically computed the spectrum of the almost Math-
ieu operator (see Section 4.3), a family of self-adjoint operators on ℓ2(Z) parametrized
by a coupling constant V ∈R and a rotation number α ∈[0, 1]. Figure 4.1 shows the
spectrum (based on numerical computations from [125]), which is known as the Hofstadter
butterfly. The spectrum is plotted only for rational values of α, since in these cases the
operator is periodic and the spectrum can be computed numerically via Floquet-Bloch
theory.
Figure 4.1: The Hofstadter butterfly taken from [125].
These numerical computations already reveal self-similar and fractal structures1 in the
spectrum, as observed earlier by Azbel [9]. The reader is also referred to recent elabora-
tions [216, 215]. Since then, the Hofstadter butterfly has attracted considerable attention
(see a more detailed discussion in Section 4.3 and Chapter 5).
Similar spectral fea-
tures and butterflies have been found in other models [191, 243, 101, 24, 25, 119, 21, 74,
187]. Most recently, Hofstadter’s butterfly was experimentally observed in twisted bilayer
graphene [187].
1Here, we only speak about numerical observations. To the best knowledge of the author, there is currently no
complete mathematically rigorous proof establishing the self-similarity of the Hofstadter butterfly. Nevertheless,
this model exhibits many other intriguing spectral features, some of which are discussed in Section 4.3 and
Chapter 5.
51


## Page 60


52
CHAPTER 4. THE WORLD OF SPECTRAL BUTTERFLIES
Another model is the so-called Kohmoto model, which is studied in further detail in this
work (see Section 4.5 and Chapter 5). It was introduced in [150] and today it serves as a toy
model for one-dimensional quasicrystals, such as those discovered by Dan Shechtman [219].
Like the almost Mathieu operator, the Kohmoto operators are parametrized by a coupling
constant V ∈R and a rotation number α ∈[0, 1], and they are periodic when α is rational.
The associated Kohmoto butterfly (based on numerical computations from [55]) is shown
in Figure 4.2. Numerically, the Kohmoto model exhibits self-similar and fractal features2
like the Hofstadter butterfly.

Figure 4.2: The Kohmoto butterfly taken from [55].
In both models – the almost Mathieu operator and the Kohmoto model – the spectral
properties of the operators with irrational α are of central interest. Since these operators
are no longer periodic, rational approximations of α are employed to study their spectral
nature; see the detailed discussion in [90] and in Chapter 5. For these models, the conver-
gence (and in some cases convergence rates) of the spectra under rational approximation
has been rigorously established [108, 78, 8, 50, 51, 47, 31].
While a variety of methods have been used – including continuous fields of C∗-algebras and
detailed analyses of approximate eigenfunctions – we highlight a unifying strategy based
on the convergence of the underlying dynamical systems. This approach was initiated in
earlier joint works [27, 29], which used C∗-algebraic techniques to prove spectral conver-
gence. In this chapter, we present recent results from the joint works [28, 41] based on
different analytic methods yielding explicit quantitative estimates. These results not only
2Here, we only speak about numerical observations. To the best knowledge of the author, there is currently
no complete mathematically rigorous proof establishing the self-similarity of the Kohmoto butterfly.


## Page 61


4.1. SPECTRAL ESTIMATES THROUGH THE UNDERLYING DYNAMICS
53
unify spectral estimates for the different models discussed so far but also show that a com-
mon mechanism governs the spectral behavior under periodic approximation. Moreover,
the spectral estimates obtained are qualitatively optimal with respect to the convergence
behavior [205, 31].
In the case of the almost Mathieu operator, a square-root behavior of spectral gaps that
close was previously observed [205] (see also a discussion in [27, 41]). In the joint work [41],
this phenomenon naturally emerges from the interaction between the amenability of the
underlying group (in this case Z) and the regularity of the operator coefficients, see also
a discussion in Remark 8.15 and Remark 8.17.
The underlying dynamical system for the almost Mathieu operator is a torus rotation. For
the Kohmoto model, the corresponding dynamical system is symbolic and, for irrational
α, these are called Sturmian dynamical system. Hence, the operators in the Kohmoto
model with irrational α are also referred to as Sturmian Hamiltonians.
We demonstrate in this section that the results of [28, 41] extend far beyond the classical
models, including:
• higher-dimensional models (see Section 4.2), with a particular emphasis on substi-
tution systems, building on results from the previous chapter,
• other models studied in the literature (e.g. the unitary almsot Mathieu operator,
see Section 4.4),
• operators defined over non-Abelian homogeneous Lie groups (see Section 4.2),
• operators over general amenable groups possibly with exponential growth, such as
the Lamplighter group [141, 174, 68], see Section 4.6.
This dynamical perspective on spectral convergence also sheds light on the structure of
the Kohmoto butterfly. In the joint work [31], spectral defects at rational α are analyzed
and previous numerical findings are confirmed rigorously. We discuss this in more detail
in Section 4.5.
Periodic approximations are used to analyze the spectral nature of the operators; see a
detailed discussion for the Kohmoto model in Chapter 5. Besides that and numerical
computations, such approximations can also be used to detect spectral gaps [52, 78, 127].
In [78, Thm. 5.1], the authors used spectral estimates to prove that all possible gaps are
there – thus solving the dry ten Martini problem for particular models.
In Section 4.1, we discuss the general results on the spectral estimates obtained in [28, 41].
In the subsequent section, we apply these results to substitution systems (Section 4.2), the
almost Mathieu operator (Section 4.3), the unitary almost Mathieu operator (Section 4.4),
the Kohmoto model (Section 4.5) and the Lamplighter group (Section 4.6).
4.1
Spectral estimates through the underlying dynamics
This section is devoted to the results of [28, 41]. In both works, spectral estimates in
terms of the distance of the underlying dynamics were established.
The work [28] focuses on symbolic dynamical systems and Schr¨odinger-type operators
acting on the Hilbert space ℓ2(G) ⊗CN, where G is a lattice in Rd and N ∈N. In this
setting we prove spectral estimates. The approach is based on localizing the resolvent
using Lipschitz-partition of unity and the convergence criterion involving local patterns,
see Proposition 3.27.
In [41], this result is generalized using approximate eigenfunctions to general topological
dynamical systems (Z, G, τ) where G is an amenable, unimodular lcsc group. This setting


## Page 62


54
CHAPTER 4. THE WORLD OF SPECTRAL BUTTERFLIES
includes the result of [28] in the case N = 1 and extends to other models such as the almost
Mathieu operator. As a consequence, it provides a unifying principle explaining the square
root behavior observed in several previously studied models. We provide here a slight
generalization of this result by allowing N to take values different from 1. Specifically,
we consider dynamically-defined operators on the Hilbert space HN := L2(G) ⊗CN for
some N ∈N. The proof extends directly following the lines of [41], and we provide the
details for completeness in the appendix, see Appendix 8.3. This slight generalization
allows us to apply our model also to the unitary or Almost-Mathieu operator discussed
in Section 4.4.
Recall the notion of a topological dynamical system (Z, G, τ) introduced in Chapter 3.
Since G is a locally compact Hausdorff group, there exists a unique left-invariant Haar
measure mG on the Borel σ-algebra of G; see, e.g., [113].
Let L2(G) := L2(G, mG)

when integrating with respect to this Haar measure mG. Throughout this section, we

Already this example includes several models studied in the literature, such as discrete
quasiperiodic Schr¨odinger operators (e.g., the almost Mathieu operator), Sturmian Hamil-
tonians, skew-shift Schr¨odinger operators, and limit-periodic Schr¨odinger operators. All
of these examples are discussed in the joint work [41], and further examples will be pro-
vided below. The reader is referred to Section 4.4 where the unitary almost Mathieu
operator is considered – an example of a dynamically-defined operator with N = 2.
In [28], we proved Lipschitz continuity of the spectral map in the following setting:
• dynamical systems (AG, G, τ) with A a compact metric space and G = AZd, where
A is an invertible d × d-matrix;
• strongly pattern-equivariant Hamiltonians (i.e., the kernel coefficients are locally
constant).
This result relies crucially on the fact that two dynamical systems are close in δA if they
agree on patches of large enough size; see Proposition 3.27. The proof adapts techniques
from [79, 80], using a Lipschitz partition of unity (adapted to the size where the patches
coincide) and resolvent estimates.
This covers, for instance, the Kohmoto model but not operators such as the almost Math-
ieu operator, which is defined by an irrational rotation on the torus. For that model,
spectral estimates in terms of the rotation number were already known [78, 8, 47].
These observations served as the starting point for [41], which aims to provide a unified
proof that also explains the differing behavior observed across models. Specifically, why
a square-root convergence rate appears in some cases but not in others, see Remark 8.17.
We return to general dynamically-defined operator families (Ax)x∈Z. The spectrum of
each operator Ax is denoted by σ(Ax), which is a compact subset of C. For a dynamical
subsystem Y ∈S of (Z, G, τ), the spectrum of the operator family AY := (Ay)y∈Y is
defined as
σ(AY ) :=

Note that the restriction of the kernel k to Y corresponds to an element of the reduced
C∗-algebra of the dynamical system (Y, G, τ). In this context, σ(AY ) coincides with the
spectrum of this element in the C∗-algebra; see e.g. [26, 29].
If each Ax is normal (i.e., Ax commutes with its adjoint), then standard arguments (see
e.g. Corollary 8.12) imply

The regularity of this

To study the spectral map, we require additional assumptions on the kernel. On the one
hand, the off-diagonals of the kernel (i.e., k(g, x) with g ̸= e) need to decay uniformly
in x ∈Z as g tends to infinity (i.e., leaves every compact set). On the other hand, the
functions k(g, ·) : Z →C must satisfy further regularity conditions.
In this context – similar to [28, 41] – we focus on kernels that satisfy a linear decay
condition and are either Lipschitz continuous or locally constant in their second argument.

An example of such a kernel is provided by the Kohmoto model or the Sturmian Hamilto-

In [41], we raised the question of whether the various spectral regularity results known
in the literature follow from a common underlying principle. This turned out to be the
case. We also provided a conceptual explanation for the well-known root-H¨older behavior
observed for the spectrum of the almost Mathieu operator [205]; see also the discussion in
[27]. This behavior can be traced back to the interaction between the amenability of the
acting group and the regularity of the kernel k; see [41] for details as well as Appendix 8.3.
Definition 4.6. The group G has strict polynomial growth if there exists constants 0 <
c0 ≤c1 and κ > 0 such that
c0rκ ≤mG(B(r)) ≤c1rκ,
for each r ≥1.
We now state the corresponding regularity result for the spectral map
Σk : S →K(C),
Y 7→σ(AY ),
where (S , δH) and (K(C), dH) are the corresponding Hausdorff metrics, introduced in
Chapter 3. The regularity of Σk is proven in the realm that the acting group G has
strictly polynomial growth.3 Note that every strictly polynomially growing group G is
amenable, see also a discussion in Appendix 8.3. In Section 4.6, we also provide spectral
estimates for the Lamplighter group that is exponentially growing but still amenable using
cut-off functions supported on an associated Følner sequence. Recall also the notion of
Lipschitz continuous dynamical system defined in Definition 3.1.
Theorem 4.7. Let (Z, G, τ) be a Lipschitz continuous dynamical system such that G is
unimodular and strictly polynomially growing. For N ∈N, let k : G × Z →CN×N be a
linearly decaying and bounded kernel. Assume Ax is a normal operator for each x ∈Z
where (Ax)x∈Z is the dynamically-defined operator family associated with k.
(a) If k is Lipschitz continuous, then the spectral map Σk is root-H¨older continuous.
More precisely, there exists a constant C := C(G, k) > 0 such that
dH (σ(AX), σ(AY )) ≤C · δH(X, Y )1/2,
for all X, Y ∈S .
(b) If k is locally constant, then the spectral map Σk is Lipschitz continuous. That is,
there exists a constant C := C(G, k) > 0 such that
dH (σ(AX), σ(AY )) ≤C · δH(X, Y ),
for all X, Y ∈S .
Proof. This is proven in [41, Thm. 1.3] for N = 1. Slight modifications of [41, Thm. 1.3]
allow to prove the statement for all N ∈N, see Appendix 8.3. Statement (b) recovers
and generalizes the results previously established in [28].
While the theorem is formulated for a broad class of groups and kernels, the obtained
regularity bounds are in fact sharp:
• The root-H¨older estimate in Theorem 4.7 (a) is known to be optimal for the almost
Mathieu operator; see [205] and a discussion in [41].
3This is a weaker assumption than the exact polynomial growth considered earlier in Section 3.3.


## Page 67


4.2. OPERATORS ASSOCIATED WITH SUBSTITUTION SYSTEMS
59
• The Lipschitz estimate in Theorem 4.7 (b) is proven to be optimal for the Kohmoto
model; see [239, 31] as well as the discussion around eq. (4.8) in Section 4.5 and
draw further conclusions from them.
In both cases the optimality of the spectral estimates is obtained at points where spectral
gaps close in the limit, see also a discussion in [27].
In the following sections, we discuss various applications of this result. Note that all
presented results deal with discrete groups G while Theorem 4.7 applies also for non-
discrete groups. For non-discrete groups, exp(−Hx) – where Hx is a Schr¨odinger type
operator – can be represented as a dynamically-defined operators, see a short discussion
in Chapter 7. The main difficulty is to prove the regularity of the kernel with respect to
the underlying dynamical system. Furthermore, first elaborations in a joint project [42]
show potential applications to frame operators where G is non-discrete as well.
4.2

for substitution systems. A version of this result can be found in [19, Thm. 2.18].

(which includes the almost Mathieu operator), such improvement has been proven in
[47]. Current investigations aim at adapting the methods from [41] to derive Lipschitz
continuity under the assumption that spectral gaps remain open, see Chapter 7.
4.4
The unitary almost Mathieu operator
In this section, we show that the spectral estimates obtained in Theorem 4.7 apply to the
unitary almost Mathieu operator. To the best of our knowledge, these estimates are new
in the literature.
The unitary almost Mathieu operator models a quantum walk in a homogeneous external
magnetic field. We refer the reader to [111, 73, 74, 72, 75] for further background and
references. Note that also the unitary almost Mathieu operator gives rise to a butterfly
and this model shares similar spectral phenomena like the almost Mathieu operator, see
[73, 74] for an illustration.
As the name suggests, this operator is closely related to the almost Mathieu operator and
is defined over the same dynamical system considered in Section 4.3. For completeness,
we briefly recall the setting.

see also [41, Sec. 2.2] and Example 4.5. Thus, Theorem 4.7 (b) applies to the operator

In this section, we focus on a particular class of operators defined over the alphabet
A := {0, 1}, sharing the same parameter space as the almost Mathieu operator discussed
in Section 4.3.
Recall that T denotes the one-dimensional torus, identified with the interval [0, 1), and
that the fractional part of a real number x ∈R is given by {x} := x −⌊x⌋; see eq. (4.2).
For a rotation parameter α ∈[0, 1] and a phase θ ∈T, we define the configuration
ωα,θ ∈AZ,

It can be shown (see e.g. [112, Sec. 6] or [96, App. 5]) that ωα,θ ∈Ωα for all θ ∈T and
that Ωα is a minimal dynamical system in the sense of Definition 3.5 for every α ∈[0, 1].
In the case where α = p
q ∈[0, 1] is rational with coprime integers p and q, the sequence
ωα,θ is periodic under the shift τ. More precisely, one has τ(q, ωα,θ) = ωα,θ for all θ ∈T,
and the orbit closure Ωα consists of exactly q distinct elements.
If α ∈[0, 1] is irrational, then ωα is a non-periodic sequence and the associated dynamical
system Ωα is referred to as a Sturmian dynamical system. For a detailed exposition and
further references, we refer the reader to [112, 204, 11, 90].
The so-called Kohmoto model [150, 192, 151, 191] is the family of discrete Schr¨odinger
operators H K
α,V,θ : ℓ2(Z) →ℓ2(Z) defined by
H K
α,V,θ := Hωα,θ,V .
The operator H K
α,V,θ is also referred to as the Sturmian Hamiltonian if α is irrational. This
is a prototypical toy model for one-dimensional quasicrystals

e.g., [169, 26] and Corollary 8.12) yield

66
CHAPTER 4. THE WORLD OF SPECTRAL BUTTERFLIES
Hence, we will omit the θ-dependence in the sequel and focus on the operator
Hα,V := H K
α,V,0
and the associated configuration ωα ∈AZ for α ∈[0, 1]. We emphasize that, in contrast
to the almost Mathieu operator, where the spectrum is θ-independent only for irrational
α, the Kohmoto model exhibits this property for all α ∈[0, 1]. This follows from the fact
that Ωα is minimal for all rational α ∈[0, 1].
Let now α = p
q ∈[0, 1] be rational with coprime integers p and q. Since ωα is q-periodic,
the spectrum σ(Hα,V ) consists of a disjoint union of q intervals for V ̸= 0, known as
spectral bands; see, e.g., [238, 225, 90], and the detailed discussion in [16, Prop. 3.5].
In general, one can only guarantee the presence of at most q spectral bands for such
periodic models. For instance, the almost Mathieu operator with α = 1
2 has only one
spectral band, see Figure 4.1. However, in the case of the Kohmoto model, one obtains
exactly q spectral bands whenever V ̸= 0. If V = 0, the potential vanishes and the
spectrum equals to [−2, 2], independently of α ∈[0, 1].
For varying rational values of α, the corresponding spectra are depicted in Figure 4.2,
illustrating the structure commonly referred to as the Kohmoto butterfly. As in the case
of the almost Mathieu operator, this butterfly structure reflects features of the spectrum
in the irrational regime. Therefore, the continuity of the spectrum at irrational α is of
central importance – a topic discussed in the following, along with further implications;
see also Chapter 5.
The mathematical foundation for the study of the Kohmoto model was laid in [70, 235,
50, 51, 207]. The spectrum of the Sturmian Hamiltonians (Hα,V with irrational α) forms
a Cantor set of Lebesgue measure zero [235, 50]. Building on earlier ideas by Casdagli
[70], Raymond [207, 208] established a coding of the spectral bands for rational α in the
regime V > 4, see a more detailed discussion in Section 5.1. A comprehensive review of
Raymond’s results was recently provided in the joint work [16].
This hierarchical structure was further extended in [18] to arbitrary couplings V ̸= 0,
which in turn allowed solving the associated dry ten Martini problem for Sturmian Hamil-
tonians; see Chapter 5 for details.
Given that the spectrum in the irrational case is a Cantor set of zero Lebesgue measure,
various fractal characteristics have been investigated in the literature; see, e.g., [207,
147, 86, 176, 94]. For a more comprehensive discussion, we refer to [83, 85, 84, 90] and
references therein.
In this section, we follow the joint work [31], where both the regularity of the spectral
map
ΣV : [0, 1] →K(R),
α 7→σ(Hα,V ),
and the emergence of spectral defects in the Kohmoto butterfly were analyzed.
Using C∗-algebraic methods, the authors of [51] proved that the spectral map ΣV is con-
tinuous at all irrational values of α, while being discontinuous at all rational α, provided
that [0, 1] is equipped with the Euclidean topology. This discontinuity is already visible
in numerical simulations – as illustrated in Figure 4.3.
Consider for example r = 2
3, for which the spectrum σ(Hr,V ) consists of three disjoint
intervals.
When approaching r from left or right, the corresponding limiting spectra
σ(Hr+,V ) and σ(Hr−,V ) exist by Theorem 4.13, and contain isolated eigenvalues located
in the spectral gaps of σ(Hr,V ), indicated by red points in Figure 4.3.
Already at the numerical level, one observes that these limiting spectra differ: the eigen-
values in σ(Hr+,V ) and σ(Hr−,V ) do not coincide. We refer to these additional eigenvalues

by building on the hierarchical structure of the spectral bands established in [207, 16, 18];
see also the discussion in Section 5.1.
To rigorously study both the spectral defects and the regularity of the spectral map,
consider the closed subspace
S := {Ωα : α ∈[0, 1]}

map [0, 1] ∋α 7→Ωα ∈S . This topology is strictly finer than the standard Euclidean
topology and – when combined with [29] – ensures the continuity of the spectral map ΣV .

The m-Farey numbers form a totally ordered set. Given r, s ∈Fm with r < s, we say

Following [31], the metric space ([0, 1], dF) is not complete. Its completion, denoted by
[0, 1]F, defines a compact metric space which we refer to as the Farey space. The topology
induced by dF is strictly finer than the Euclidean topology – that is, convergence in the
Farey metric implies convergence in the usual Euclidean sense.
In [31, Cor. 3.4], we showed that the Farey space [0, 1]F can be realized by adjoining to
each rational number r ∈[0, 1] a left- and right-sided limit point r−and r+, respectively:
[0, 1]F = [0, 1] ∪{r−| r ∈Q ∩(0, 1]} ∪{r+ | r ∈Q ∩[0, 1)}.
Moreover, by [31, Thm. 3.8], there exists a unique surjective isometry
Φ : [0, 1]F →S
satisfying Φ(α) = Ωα for all α ∈[0, 1].

## Page 81


5
The dry ten Martini problem for Sturmian
Hamiltonians
During the AMS Annual Meeting in 1981, Mark Kac asked whether the almost Mathieu
operator “has all its gaps there” [223] for irrational α, offering ten Martinis to whoever
could solve the problem. Shortly thereafter, Barry Simon [223] coined the name of the
(dry) ten Martini problem.
The question is closely tied to the study of spectral gaps in quasiperiodic operators and
the so-called Gap Labeling Theorem, which predicts the set of possible gap labels for the
spectrum based on the underlying dynamical system. In the case of the almost Mathieu
operator, it was conjectured that the spectrum is a Cantor set and that all allowed gaps
are there – that is, the spectrum realizes the maximal number of spectral bands separated
by actual gaps.
A closely related question also remained a long-standing open problem for the Sturmian
Hamiltonians – a prototypical toy model for one-dimensional quasicrystals. It was recently
fully resolved in the joint work [18].
We discuss these problems in parallel for both the almost Mathieu operator and the
Sturmian Hamiltonians, highlighting their structural similarities.
For α ∈[0, 1] \ Q and V ∈R, recall the definitions of the almost Mathieu operator
H A
α,V,0 : ℓ2(Z) →ℓ2(Z) from Section 4.3 and the Kohmoto model H K
α,V,0 : ℓ2(Z) →ℓ2(Z)
from Section 4.5.1 Recall that H K
α,V,0 is also called a Sturmian Hamiltonian if α is irrational.
In the following, we write Hα,V to refer to either H A
α,V,0 or H K
α,V,0.
The ten Martini problem (TMP): One asks whether the spectrum σ(Hα,V ) is a Cantor
set for V ̸= 0 and irrational α ∈[0, 1] \ Q.
For the almost Mathieu operator, the final solution of the TMP was given by the break-
through work of Avila and Jitomirskaya [3], building on a long line of partial results and
techniques developed in [52, 226, 132, 78, 158, 203, 5].
For the Sturmian Hamiltonians, it is known that the spectrum is a Cantor set of zero
Lebesgue measure [235, 50]. Substantial generalizations were established in [162, 97, 99]
based on the so-called Boshernitzan condition [63], which was further extended to Jacobi
operators in the joint work [39]. A key tool in this analysis is Kotani theory [224, 153, 154].

The possible gap labels are described by the Gap Labeling Theorem [44, 138, 46, 49, 89, 91],
which can be approached via different frameworks. One approach, originally developed
by Jean Bellissard, is based on K-theory. For one-dimensional models such as Hα,V , an
alternative approach is due to Johnson and Moser.
For both the almost Mathieu operator and the Sturmian Hamiltonians, one obtains the
same set of possible gap labels, namely
{Nα,V (E) : E ∈R \ σ(Hα,V )} ⊆{{nα} : n ∈Z} ∪{1},
α ∈[0, 1] \ Q,
where {nα} := nα −⌊nα⌋denotes the fractional part of nα. With this in hand, we can
formally state the question originally raised by Kac: When do we have equality in the
above inclusion for irrational α? That is, does there exist, for each n ∈Z, a spectral gap
g ⊆R \ σ(Hα,V ) such that Nα,V (E) = {nα} for all E ∈g? Note that the possible gap
labels are dense in [0, 1], if α ∈[0, 1] is irrational. Also the gap label 1 is always attained
for any energy above the supremum of the spectrum.
Substantial progress toward resolving this question for the almost Mathieu operator has
been made over the past decades [78, 203, 4, 178], and most recently in [6].


## Page 83


75
While the question was originally formulated for the almost Mathieu operator, it has since
been studied for a wide range of other models; see, for instance, [124, 100, 87, 75, 118].
We emphasize, however, that the set of possible gap labels can vary depending on the
underlying model.
For the Sturmian Hamiltonians, the set of possible gap labels coincides with that of the
almost Mathieu operator. The solution of the DTMP for the Sturmian Hamiltonians was
achieved in the joint work [18]; see also the corresponding Oberwolfach preprint [17]. A
first significant step in this direction was taken by Raymond in his PhD thesis [208], see
also [207]. This preprint had a notable influence on the field (see e.g. [83, 85, 84, 90]
and references therein), and a modernized presentation of these ideas is provided in the
joint work [16], adapted to the perspective of [18]. Raymond’s results will also appear in
revised form in [206].
In his work, Raymond classified the spectral bands of periodic approximations into three
types under the assumption V > 4. The resulting coding allowed him to express the
IDS explicitly (see Proposition 5.14) and thereby to resolve the DTMP for the Sturmian
Hamiltonians if V > 4.
The case V > 4 corresponds to the regime in which the potential dominates the kinetic
term of the Schr¨odinger operator. Similar assumptions – often even stronger – appear
in the literature when analyzing the fractal nature of the spectrum. The main technical
difficulty beyond this regime is the overlap of spectral band in different approximations
and the lack of techniques to control such overlaps.
A particular focus in the literature has been placed on the case where α equals the inverse
of the golden mean
ϕ :=
√
5 −1
2
=
 √
5 + 1
2
!−1
,
whose continued fraction expansion consists entirely of ones. The associated operator
H K
ϕ,V,0 is commonly referred to as the Fibonacci Hamiltonian, since its rational approxi-
mations are determined by the Fibonacci numbers.
Damanik and Gorodetski [92] showed that, for the Fibonacci Hamiltonian, all predicted
gap labels appear if the coupling constant V is sufficiently small. This technique was
extended by Mei [183] to irrational α with eventually periodic continued fraction expan-
sions. In this setting, the threshold for how small the coupling must be depends on the
continued fraction expansion of the given irrational number. Later, Damanik, Gorodetski,
and Yessen [94] proved – among many other remarkable results – that all spectral gaps are
there for the Fibonacci Hamiltonian solving the DTMP for the Fibonacci Hamiltonian.
Note that the golden mean, as well as irrational numbers with eventually periodic contin-
ued fraction expansions, satisfy a Diophantine condition excluding many other irrational
numbers.
A unifying theme in the aforementioned results is the study of traces of the transfer
matrices and their recursive relations. While these algebraic relations provide deep insight,
they are not sufficient on their own to resolve the DTMP for the Sturmian Hamiltonians.
In our joint work [18], two new ingredients – allowing us to control also overlaps of spectral
bands – were introduced:
• We studied the space of all continued fraction expansions simultaneously, rather
than fixing a single irrational α.
• We employed interlacing arguments for eigenvalues to control spectral bands in the
small coupling regime 0 < V ≤4.

Remark 5.2. By standard arguments, Theorem 5.1 also implies that σ(H K
α,V,0) is a Cantor
set for all α ∈[0, 1] \ Q; see the brief discussion in Remark 5.13. That the spectrum is a
Cantor set – indeed, even of Lebesgue measure zero – was previously established in [50].
Theorem 5.1 thus provides an alternative proof of the Cantor structure of the spectrum.
In the following, we provide an overview of the strategy used to prove this result, as well
as additional results obtained along the way.
5.1
Classifying the spectral bands of periodic approximations
The first step in the proof is to classify the spectral bands of periodic approximations.
This classification is of independent interest and has various other applications. Inspired
by the work of Casdagli [70], Raymond [207] encoded the spectral bands of H K
α,V,0 for
rational α ∈[0, 1] ∩Q and V > 4 into three types: a spectral gap (type I), and two types
of spectral bands (type II and type III). Inspired by this work, a tree representation of the
periodic approximations was developed in [18]. In [16], we revisit Raymond’s classification
including the new perspectives from the joint work [18]. Only two types of spectral bands
are used: type A (corresponding to type III) and type B (corresponding to type II) – a
notion also used before for the Fibonacci Hamiltonian [147].
This classification depends on the finite continued fraction expansion of the rational num-
ber α. Recall that every α ∈[0, 1] defines a symbolic subshift Ωα ⊆AZ with A := {0, 1},
see eq. (4.6) in Section 4.5. Moreover, as discussed in that section, the spectrum of H K
α,V,0
for irrational α ∈[0, 1] \ Q can be approximated by the spectra of H K
β,V,0 with rational
β ∈[0, 1] ∩Q; see Corollary 4.14.

Remark 5.3. Let A := N−1 ∪{∞} be the one-point compactification of N−1.
This
compact alphabet can be used to describe the Farey space (excluding the rational number
itself) as discussed in Section 4.5. Every irrational α ∈[0, 1] \ Q can be identified with
its unique infinite continued fraction [0, 0, c1, c2, c3, . . .], and thus embedded in the product
space X := {x : N−1 →A}. Each c ∈C is embedded into X by continuing the finite
tuple with the constant letter ∞. Let Y ⊆X be the subset consisting of all such embedded
c ∈C and all irrational numbers, equipped with the induced product topology.
By the previous considerations, every rational r ∈[0, 1] ∩Q appears with two continued
fraction expansions, which can be interpreted as left and right limit points r−, r+ of r.
Indeed, the sequence xm := [c, m, ∞, ∞, . . .] converges to [c, ∞, ∞, . . .] in the product
topology. The same convergents were used in [31] to describe spectral defects.

Returning to Proposition 5.5, we conclude that for V > 4, each spectral band I(V ) ⊆
σc(V ) is strictly contained either in a spectral band of σ[c,0](V ) or of σ[c,−1](V ). This
characterizes the spectral bands as one of two types. Here, an interval [a, b] is said to be
strictly contained in [c, d] if
[a, b] ⊆strict [c, d]
:⇔
c < a ≤b < d.
In [207], only non-strict inclusions of spectral bands were studied. The refinement to strict
inclusions is essential when extending these ideas to the small coupling regime 0 < V ≤4.
Recall from the definition of the evaluation map φ that appending a “0” to a finite
continued fraction c ∈C effectively deletes its last digit – this operation characterizes
type A.
In contrast, appending a “−1” reduces the last digit by one; repeating this
procedure until the last digit becomes zero corresponds to deleting the last two digits
under the evaluation map – this defines type B.
This inclusion-based behavior provides a natural classification of spectral bands into two
types, as illustrated in panel (a) of Figure 5.2. Each spectral band from a given approxi-
mation αk = φ(ck) of an irrational α can thus be assigned a type based on its containment
in the bands of previous approximations2.
Definition 5.7 (Type A and B). Let V ̸= 0, α ∈[0, 1] \ Q with continued fraction
expansion [0, 0, c1, c2, . . .], the kth convergent ck = [0, 0, c1, . . . , ck] ∈C and αk = φ(ck)
for k ∈N. A spectral band Ick(V ) ⊆σck(V ) is called
• of type A if Ick(V ) ⊆strict J for some spectral band J ⊆σck−1(V ),
• of type B if Ick(V ) ⊆strict J for some spectral band J ⊆σck−2(V ) and Ick(V ) is not
contained in any spectral band of σck−1(V ).
Note that the definition of spectral band types provided here differs from the formulation
in [18, 16], but is equivalent; see [18, Prop. 7.19]. Establishing this equivalence for arbi-
trary V ̸= 0 is non-trivial and requires proving first that every spectral band possesses

q ∈[0, 1], where p and q are coprime integers. Recall that
for V ̸= 0, the spectrum σc(V ) consists of exactly q disjoint intervals, each referred to as
a spectral band and denoted Ic(V ).
Following [18, Def. 1.5], we extend the notion of a spectral band by exploiting the con-
tinuity of the map V 7→σc(V ); see, e.g., [18, Cor. 3.2]. More precisely, for a fixed index
0 ≤j < q, let Ic(V ) denote the j-th spectral band in σc(V ), counted from left to right.
Then the map
(0, ∞) ∋V 7→Ic(V )
is continuous in the Hausdorff metric, meaning the band edges vary continuously with V .
With a slight abuse of terminology, we will also refer to the function (0, ∞) ∋V 7→Ic(V )
as a spectral band. An analogous statement holds for negative coupling constants V < 0.
Since the spectral bands vary continuously in V , the strict inclusion relations that de-
fine their type cannot change as long as V > 4 using the three-intersection property in
Proposition 5.5. Thus, the type of each spectral band remains invariant in this regime.
However, for V ≤4, overlaps between spectral bands at different approximation levels
may occur, as illustrated in Example 5.6. These overlaps can lead to changes or even loss
of type, presenting a major conceptual difficulty.
A central achievement of [18] is to overcome this issue by proving that every spectral
band has a well-defined and consistent type for all V ̸= 0, as formulated in the following
theorem.


## Page 89


5.1. CLASSIFYING THE SPECTRAL BANDS
81
Theorem 5.8. For all V ̸= 0 and all c ∈C with φ(c) ̸∈{−1, ∞}, each spectral band
Ic(V ) ⊆σc(V ) is of either type A or type B, and its type remains invariant for all V > 0,
respectively all V < 0.
Proof. For the case V > 4, the result was shown by Raymond [207]; see also [16]. The
general case V ̸= 0 was proven in [18, Thm. 2.15, Cor. 7.7]. Conceptually, the latter proof
relies on the following key ingredients:
• the recursive structure of trace polynomials of the transfer matrices via Chebyshev
polynomials [18, Sec. 4.6],
• interlacing properties of spectral band edges for admissible pairs of spectral band
edges [18, Thm. 3.4],
• uniform (in c ∈C ) Lipschitz continuity of band edges [18, Cor. 3.2],
• and a two-level induction over the space C of finite continued fraction expansions [18,
Sec. 5 and 6].
This classification has also been employed to estimate the fractal dimension of the spec-
trum [147, 177, 86, 92, 176, 93, 69, 179]. Therefore it is interesting in itself as mentioned
in Section 4.5 before.
At this point, we emphasize again that the type of a spectral band Ic(V ) ⊆σc(V ) depends
on the specific continued fraction expansion c, and not merely on its evaluation φ(c);
see [18, Lem. 2.10]. However, the rational approximations αk of an irrational α ∈[0, 1]
have a unique finite continued fraction expansion given by the kth convergent ck.
Following Definition 5.7, the type of a spectral band Ick(V ) is determined by whether it is
strictly contained in a band at level k −1 or level k −2. Moreover, knowing the next digit
ck+1 of the continued fraction expansion allows us to determine how many spectral bands
of type A and type B appear inside Ick(V ) at the following two levels. This condition is
referred to as forward type in [18, 16]. These bands are interlaced in a precise way, for
which we define the order relation3
[a, b] ≺[c, d]

Proof. For V > 4, the result was shown in [207, Lem. 3.3]; see also [16, Lem. 4.14]. The
general case V ̸= 0 was established in [18, Thm. 2.15, Cor. 7.7] using the equivalent notion
of types defined in [18, Prop. 7.19]. Note that the formal definition of forward type in
[18, 16] does not require the uniqueness of the M spectral bands of type A and the M +1

a counting argument based on the total number of spectral bands.
An illustration of this forward type structure is provided in panel (b) of Figure 5.3. As

0 < V ≤4. If V > 4, such overlaps do not occur by the three-intersection property
(Proposition 5.5), and a stricter interlacing property holds.
Proposition 5.9 shows that the continued fraction expansion of α uniquely determines
how many spectral bands of type A and type B are found in each spectral band at level
k, together with their relative position. This provides a combinatorial encoding of the
spectral structure, which will serve as the foundation for the tree construction discussed
below; see also Figure 5.2.
5.2
The spectrum as the boundary of the spectral tree
The forward classification of spectral bands described in Proposition 5.9 naturally leads
to a hierarchical and recursive organization of spectral data. This structure can be en-
coded in a rooted tree, referred to as the spectral α-tree associated with a fixed irrational
number α ∈[0, 1] \ Q. Each level k in this tree corresponds to the rational approximation


## Page 91


5.2. THE SPECTRAL TREE
83
αk = φ(ck), and its vertices represent the spectral bands in σck(V ), as determined by
Theorem 5.8 and Proposition 5.9.
Figure 5.2 and Figure 5.3 illustrate the core idea behind the construction: each spectral
band at level k gives rise to a fixed number of type A and type B spectral bands at the next
levels, encoded via the branching of the tree. This spectral α-tree retains all combinatorial
information relevant to the associated integrated density of state; see Proposition 5.14. At
the same time, it omits non-essential details such as the precise positions of band edges.
Importantly, the spectral α-tree is entirely independent of the coupling constant V > 0;
its structure is governed solely by the continued fraction expansion of α.
We begin by recalling the formal notion of a directed graph, as previously used in Sec-
tion 3.6.
A tuple G = (V, E) is called a directed graph if V is a countable set (the vertex set) and
E ⊆V × V is a set of directed edges. An element (u, w) ∈E is a directed edge from u to
w, with (u, w) ̸= (w, u) in general.
A (directed) path in G is a finite or infinite sequence of vertices (u0, u1, u2, . . .) such that
(ui, ui+1) ∈E for all i ∈N0. For two vertices u, w ∈V, we write u →w if there exists
a finite path (u, u1, . . . , um, w) from u to w. A path (u0, u1, . . . , um) is called a cycle if
u0 = um and m ∈N. A directed graph is called a tree if it contains no cycles. It is called
a rooted tree if there exists a unique vertex r ∈V (the root) such that for all v ∈V, there
exists a path r →v. A rooted tree is called an ordered tree if the vertex set V carries a
strict (i.e., irreflexive) partial order relation ≺.
Motivated by Proposition 5.9, we now define the spectral α-tree, which organizes the
spectral data in a recursive and hierarchical manner; see the illustration in Figure 5.3.
Definition 5.10 (Spectral α-tree). Let α ∈[0, 1] \ Q with continued fraction expansion
[0, 0, c1, c2, . . .]. The spectral α-tree Tα = (Vα, Eα) is defined recursively as follows:
• Let r ∈Vα be the root at level k = −1.
• Add a vertex u0 ∈Vα at level k = 0 with label A, and an edge (r, u0) ∈Eα.
• Add a vertex w0 ∈Vα at level k = 1 with label B, and an edge (r, w0) ∈Eα.
• Declare the initial order relation u0 ≺w0.
Now, recursively extend the tree: For each vertex v ∈Vα at level k ≥0, set
M :=

Then:
• Add M vertices u1, . . . , uM ∈Vα at level k + 1 with label A, and connect each via
(v, ui) ∈Eα for 1 ≤i ≤M.
• Add M + 1 vertices w1, . . . , wM+1 ∈Vα at level k + 2 with label B, and connect each
via (v, wj) ∈Eα for 1 ≤j ≤M + 1.
• Define the strict order relation on these new vertices as4
w1 ≺u1 ≺w2 ≺. . . ≺uM ≺wM+1.
4In particular, we have ui ≺ui+1 and wj ≺wj+1 by transitivity.

Theorem 5.12 ([18], Encoding of the spectrum). Let α ∈[0, 1] \ Q. Then the map
Eα : ∂Tα × (0, ∞) →σ(H K
α,V,0),

Proof. This result is proven in [18, Thm. 1.9]. Properties (b) and (c) follow directly from
the nested structure of spectral bands established in Proposition 5.11 and the monotone
convergence from Proposition 5.4. The surjectivity in (a) is a direct consequence of the
convergence of the spectrum.
The central difficulty lies in proving injectivity of the map. This is resolved by a refined
analysis of how spectral bands intersect in the critical regime V ≤4, building on the classi-
fication of band types from Theorem 5.8 and an extended version of the three-intersection
property in Proposition 5.5. For details, we refer to the comprehensive argumentation in
[18, Thm. 1.9].

α,V,0) is a Cantor set of Lebesgue

Hamiltonians. We use this result only once in [18], as mentioned above – specifically,
to show that Iγ(V ) is a singleton. However, the application of the TMP for Sturmian
Hamiltonians can be avoided at this point, as follows:

By construction, Ψ(um)(V ) is a spectral band of a rational approximation pm
qm of α, where
pm and qm are coprime. The length of such a spectral band can be estimated by

e.g., [90, Thm 7.5.1].
Since α is irrational, the denominators qm tend to infinity as

Iγ(V ) consists of a single point.
This argument shows that one can establish the solution of the DTMP for Sturmian Hamil-
tonians without relying on the fact that the spectrum is a Cantor set of Lebesgue measure
zero. Since the gap labels (for irrational α) are dense in [0, 1], the DTMP implies the
TMP by standard arguments, see, e.g., [78, Rem 5.2]. Specifically, Theorem 5.1 implies
that the spectrum σ(H K
α,V,0) is a Cantor set for all α ∈[0, 1] \ Q. Thus, [18] also provides
an alternative proof of this result.
Comparison to existing descriptions of the spectrum. Theorem 5.12 shows that
the spectral tree Tα fully encodes the spectrum of the operator H K
α,V,0 in terms of its
boundary. Various strategies have been employed in the literature (see [90, Thm. 10.5.2])
to characterize the spectrum σ(H K
α,V,0) of the Sturmian Hamiltonians:
• The set Z of energies where the Lyapunov exponent vanishes plays a key role in
proving that σ(H K
α,V,0) is a Cantor set of zero Lebesgue measure. This is based on
Kotani theory [224, 153, 154] and was developed in works such as [235, 50, 162, 97,
99], see also a discussion in [88, Sec. 4.8].
• The set B of energies for which the positive orbit under the associated trace map
remains bounded has been used to analyze the fractal geometry of the spectrum,
including its Hausdorff dimension; see [70, 207, 86, 94].
• A symbolic coding scheme Π, originally inspired by the trace map analysis of Cas-
dagli [70] and further developed by Raymond [207] for V > 4 was used to prove
that all spectral gaps are there if V > 4. This coding also provides insight into the
fractal geometry of the spectrum and related quantities such as transport exponents
[147, 177, 86, 92, 176, 93, 69, 179]. Coding methods have also been applied to other
substitution models, e.g., the period-doubling sequence in [175].
In [18], the perspective was changed replacing the symbolic coding Π with the boundary
of a spectral tree ∂Tα; see also a discussion in [16].

With all preparations in place, Theorem 5.1 now follows from the preceding considerations;

Using Proposition 5.14, one explicitly constructs a path
η = (u0, u1, u2, . . .) ∈∂Tα such that

This path is constructed so that the associated summands (see eq. (5.2)) in Nα

2. The path η is then modified to a second path γ ∈∂Tα such that Nα(γ) = Nα(η),

and
Nα,V (Eα(γ; V )) = {nα} = Nα,V (Eα(η; V )).
Hence, property (IDS–1) implies that the integrated density of states remains con-
stant on the interval (Eα(γ; V ), Eα(η; V )). By property (IDS–2), this interval lies
entirely in the resolvent set and thus forms a spectral gap. The value {nα} is then
the corresponding gap label.
Let us conclude this chapter with a brief discussion on how to label or color the Kohmoto
butterfly, now that Theorem 5.1 ensures that all spectral gaps are open.
Let α ∈[0, 1] be irrational. Then, by Theorem 5.1, for each fractional part {nα}, there
exists a spectral gap g of H K
α,V,0 such that

where k, m ∈Z are uniquely determined due to the irrationality of α. In the following,
we refer to the integer k as the gap index of g.
In the context of the almost Mathieu operator, this integer k corresponds to the Hall
conductance [190]. In that work, all spectral gaps are labeled by identifying each gap
index k with a distinct color. We refer the reader to [190] for further background and
references.
Let us now return to the Kohmoto butterfly. At first glance, the butterfly seems to consist
of connected spectral gaps (white regions in Figure 4.2). However, as emphasized in [31]
and discussed in Section 4.5, the Euclidean topology on [0, 1] is not adequate for analyzing
the Kohmoto model. Instead, one should consider the finer Farey topology in which [0, 1]
is cut at every rational value disconnecting the spectral gaps.
As previously mentioned, the butterfly is generated numerically by computing the spectra
for rational values of α.
Consequently, only these spectral gaps can be colored.

## Page 99


6
Generalized eigenfunctions of Schr¨odinger
operators
In Chapter 4, we presented several results to approximate the spectrum by the spectra of
other operators, which was employed in Chapter 5. We now return to the more general
setting of Schr¨odinger-type operators in both the continuous and discrete framework. In
this chapter, we present results from the joint works [38, 32], where we analyze when a gen-
eralized eigenfunction gives rise to an element of the spectrum. In the subsequent section,
we characterize the essential spectrum of Schr¨odinger operators on graphs, following the
joint work [33]. Note that discrete Schr¨odinger operators associated with Delone sets fall
into this framework of Schr¨odinger operators on graphs; see [40, Sec. 5] and Example 6.6.
Under suitable assumptions on the underlying space, the results of this chapter can be
summarized as follows:
• The spectrum σ(H) of a Schr¨odinger-type operator can be characterized by general-
ized eigenfunctions whose growth is either controlled by the geometry (e.g., subex-
ponential growth) or by an intrinsic object such as the Agmon ground state; see
[38, 32] and Section 6.1.
• The essential spectrum σess(H) is characterized by bounded generalized eigenfunc-
tions under suitable growth conditions of the underlying graph; see [33] and Sec-
tion 6.2.
6.1
The spectrum of Schr¨odinger-type operators: Shnol type
theorem
This section follows the joint works [38, 32], which address the question under which
conditions a generalized eigenvalue λ, associated with a generalized eigenfunction of a
self-adjoint operator H, belongs to the spectrum of H.
Before introducing the formal framework, we briefly outline the background of this ques-
tion. Shnol [220] showed that for a Schr¨odinger operator H on Rd with a potential bounded
from below, any generalized eigenfunction with at most polynomial growth implies that
the corresponding energy lies in the spectrum of H; see also [222]. Furthermore, it was
shown in [221, 222, 82] that there exists a dense set of spectral values admitting such
generalized eigenfunctions.
Extensions to the framework of Dirichlet forms were provided in [65, 64, 122, 116], where
the polynomial growth condition is replaced by a subexponential growth requirement for
the generalized eigenfunction.
The underlying proofs use:
• a Weyl sequence argument (approximate eigenfunctions),1
1These methods were also applied in [41], see Section 4.1 and Appendix 8.3.
91


## Page 100


92
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED EIGENFUNCTIONS
• suitable cut-off functions, called admissible cut-off sequences in [32],
• local gradient estimates – so-called Caccioppoli type estimates [57, 129, 64].
The work [38] was motivated by the conjecture stated in [102, Conj. 9.9], which asks
whether the polynomial growth condition can be replaced by a bound involving the Agmon
ground state of the Schr¨odinger operator (provided it exists). In [32], a more general
framework was developed that unifies various growth bounds under a single concept. It
is also shown that the resulting condition is close to optimal (see [32, Rem. 1.5]).
Additionally, [32, Exam. 7.3] provides examples of generalized eigenfunctions exhibiting
exponential growth but where one can still prove with similar techniques that the gener-
alized eigenvalue lies in the spectrum of the operator.

the spectrum of HV . Examples illustrating this result can be found in [38, Sec. 1].

The essential spectrum and bounded generalized eigenfunc-
tions
This section follows [33], where the essential spectrum of discrete Schr¨odinger operators
on graphs is analyzed in terms of generalized eienfunctions.

Recall the notion of a weighted graph (X, b) introduced in Definition 6.4. Such a graph
is called combinatorial if the edge weights b take only values in {0, 1}. In this case, the
relation y ∼x defined by b(x, y) = 1 induces a symmetric relation on X. A graph (X, b)
is said to be d-bounded if the vertex degree is uniformly bounded by d ∈N, that is,
sup
x∈X
deg(x) ≤d.
Thus, a d-bounded combinatorial graph has uniformly bounded vertex degrees. Through-
out this section, we fix the weight m : X →(0, ∞) to be identically one.
Let (X, b) be a combinatorial d-bounded graph and let c : X →[0, ∞) be a bounded
potential. We consider the associated self-adjoint operator Hc defined by
(Hcu)(x) :=
X
y∼x


+ c(x)u(x),
u ∈ℓ2(X).
The discrete spectrum σdisc(Hc) is defined as the set of isolated eigenvalues of Hc with
finite multiplicity. The essential spectrum is then given by
σess(Hc) := σ(Hc) \ σdisc(Hc).
By Weyl’s theorem, the essential spectrum is stable under compact perturbations.
Recall the notion of generalized eigenfunctions introduced in the previous section. There,
we established that if such functions exhibit sufficiently slow growth, the corresponding
generalized eigenvalue lies in the spectrum σ(Hc). In the characterization of the essential
spectrum, only bounded generalized eigenfunctions are relevant under suitable growth
assumptions of the graph. We define the set
σ∞(Hc) :=

λ ∈C :
λ is a generalized eigenvalue of
a bounded generalized eigenfunction

.
We have previously encountered the essential spectrum in the context of systems with
purely essential spectrum, a concept that is intimately related to minimality. As shown
in [169, 26], minimality is equivalent to the constancy of the spectrum across the entire
dynamical system, and this common spectrum coincides with the essential spectrum. For
weighted Delone sets in G, minimality is further characterized by repetitivity; see [35,
Prop. 2.4] and [117] for unweighted Delone sets. Roughly speaking, repetitivity means
that every finite pattern (or patch) appears relatively densely in the Delone set, i.e., with
uniformly bounded gaps. Hence, repetitive weighted Delone sets exhibit no dynamical
impurities, meaning that no patch is confined to a region of the space without recurring
throughout with bounded gaps.
Minimality implies, even for non-normal operators, that the spectrum is constant; see
[36, 37] for further discussion. This phenomenon underlines the intrinsic relation between
the spectral type and the global topological dynamical structure of the system.
The underlying philosophy of these results is that the essential spectrum captures the
behavior ”at infinity” of the space or structure in question. In [33], we established such a
result for d-bounded combinatorial graphs. Earlier results of this type were obtained for
operators on N or Zd; see [77, 225]. There, the concept of right limits plays a central role.
Informally, a right limit is a limit point in the shift space of the system that corresponds
to the strong limit of a sequence of shifted operators. This notion was extended to higher
dimensions in [159], and to general d-bounded graphs in [66], where it was adapted to the
concept of R-limits.


## Page 107


6.2. THE ESSENTIAL SPECTRUM
99
We omit the technical definition of R-limits here and refer the reader to [33, Def. 4.2]. It
suffices to note that the set R(Hc) of all R-limits consists of bounded self-adjoint operators
acting on possibly different graphs (X′, b′) that arise as a limits of (X, b) shifting a root.
In [66], it was proven that the union of the spectra of R-limits contains the essential
spectrum of Hc. Moreover, they showed that the reverse inclusion holds for regular trees.
On the other hand, they also provided an example of an infinite, connected, d-bounded
graph for which the equality fails. This observation served as a motivation for the joint
work [33].
A rooted graph (X, b, v) is a graph (X, b) together with a distinguished vertex v ∈X. The
ball of radius r ∈N centered at v is defined by
Br(v) :=

u ∈X :
∃n ≤r, ∃x0, . . . , xn ∈X with
x0 = v, xn = u, xi−1 ∼xi for all 1 ≤i ≤n


which is the ball with respect to the combinatorial graph distance. The rooted graph
(X, b, v) is said to have subexponential growth if for every γ > 0, there exists a constant
C = C(γ, v) > 0 such that
♯Br(v) < Cγr,
r ∈N,
where ♯Br(v) denotes the number of elements in Br(v). If the constant C(γ, v) can be
chosen uniformly in v ∈X, the graph (X, b) is said to have uniform subexponential growth,
i.e. supv∈X C(γ, v) < ∞for all γ > 0.

## Page 109


7
Outlook
Throughout this work, we have highlighted several open questions, which we briefly re-
visit here. The primary focus lies on potential spectral applications in higher-dimensional
settings. We also discuss possible future uses of the spectral tree construction developed
for the Kohmoto model. In certain contexts, it may be of independent interest to inves-
tigate extensions of the presented results to broader classes of operators, more general
dynamical systems, or frameworks beyond the specific setting studied in this work. The
topics are organized according to the chapter structure of this thesis.
Linearly repetitive Delone sets
In [34], we constructed a class of symbolic dynamical systems over non-Abelian groups
that exhibit linear repetitivity. To the best of our knowledge, these represent the first
non-trivial examples in this setting. From these symbolic systems, one can also derive
linearly repetitive Delone sets.
An interesting question is whether there exist geometric constructions of linearly repetitive
Delone sets in homogeneous Lie groups that do not arise from symbolic substitutions. One
possible approach would be to formulate a suitable notion of geometric substitutions for
these groups.
Another promising direction lies in the theory of cut-and-project sets, where linear repet-
itivity is well understood in the Euclidean case [126, 152]. It remains to be seen whether
similar arguments can be extended to non-Abelian settings.
Compared to substitution systems, the greater structural flexibility of cut-and-project
sets offers additional possibilities for constructing periodic approximations.
Following
[104], periodic approximations were provided for the octagonal tiling by exploiting its
substitutive structure; see the discussion in Section 3.7. The initial periodic configuration
was constructed using a symmetry of the underlying cut-and-project set. It remains an
open question whether a general approximation scheme can be developed for a broader
class of cut-and-project sets. In particular, it is unknown whether this approach can yield
periodic approximations for the Penrose tiling.
If not, one should study the nature of possible dynamical impurities and spectral defects
in analogy to our analysis of the Kohmoto model; see [31] and the discussion in Sec-
tion 4.5. Recent developments [126, 152] providing characterizations of linear repetitivity
for regular model sets may serve as a promising starting point for further investigations.
Higher-dimensional models
A central motivation for the approximation method developed in this work is the hope
that it provides access to the spectral theory of higher-dimensional aperiodic Schr¨odinger
operators, where currently only limited results are available. A natural next step is to
investigate which additional spectral properties can be recovered or passed to the limit.

CHAPTER 7. OUTLOOK
More precisely, let An be a sequence of dynamically defined operators. We have studied
whether the spectra σ(An) converge to the spectrum σ(A) of a limiting operator A, as
discussed in Chapter 4. In this context, each operator An (respectively A) is defined via
a kernel on a dynamical system Xn (respectively X), and we assume that the sequence
of dynamical systems (Xn)n∈N converges to X.
However, spectral convergence alone does not generally imply convergence of other spec-
tral properties. For instance, convergence of the spectra does not guarantee convergence
of the Lebesgue measure. In fact, only the inequality
lim sup
n→∞λ(σ(An)) ≤λ(σ(A))
holds in general where λ denotes the Lebesgue measure. Additional structure, such as
monotonic convergence (discussed below), can imply convergence of the Lebesgue mea-
sure. Alternatively, quantitative control on spectral convergence combined with infor-
mation on the number of spectral gaps may also lead to convergence of the Lebesgue
measure. A particular application can be found in [157, Thm. 2] and [158, Thm. 1] for
the almost Mathieu operator, see also a discussion in [237].
To explore these questions, we initiated a joint project [20] using numerical computa-
tions.
These computations indicate new phenomena that have not been observed in
one-dimensional models:
• Consider the table tiling and its periodic approximations as constructed in Proposi-
tion 3.54 (a), where the associated dynamical systems converge. The spectral bands
rapidly become extremely thin. Numerically, the spectrum appears to vanish after
only a few iterations. If one could confirm that the Lebesgue measures of the spectra
converge and that the limit has zero measure, this would imply that the spectrum
of the associated Schr¨odinger operator has zero Lebesgue measure as well.
• In contrast, the single-letter approximations discussed in Proposition 3.54 (b) yield
dynamical systems that do not converge. In the numerics, these approximations
lead to substantially thicker spectra, raising the question of how large the deviation
in the limit can be when using poorly chosen approximations.
In the forthcoming work [20] (see also [237]), we demonstrate that for specific Schr¨odinger
operators, the limiting essential spectra may differ if one takes poor approximations. In
one-dimensional systems, only compact perturbations are possible, so differences in the
limit can affect at most the discrete spectrum. In higher dimensions, however, we show
that the limiting Lebesgue measure itself may even change.
Our methods build on the computation of spectral limits and identification of dynamical
impurities, in the spirit of [31] for the Kohmoto model; see also Section 4.5. In higher
dimensions, these impurities may take the form of infinite strips, which can perturb the
essential spectrum and even alter its Lebesgue measure.
These observations mark only the beginning of a range of interesting open problems.
Monotonicity of the Spectrum
In Chapter 5, we studied the classification of spectral bands arising from approximations
of a specific aperiodic Schr¨odinger operator. This classification was based on tracking
whether spectral bands from a given approximation were contained in those of earlier
ones; see Proposition 5.4. While this provides a first structural insight, a more detailed


## Page 111


103
understanding of the spectral bands requires additional information; see [207, 208, 16, 18].
Nevertheless, the observed monotonicity of the spectrum, i.e.,
σ(An) ⊆σ(An−1) ∪σ(An−2),
serves as a key guiding principle at an initial stage.
Analogous hierarchical structures and codings have been investigated for other models,
such as the period-doubling chain [175].
In Chapter 4, we observed that spectral convergence is tightly linked to the convergence of
the underlying dynamical systems. This naturally leads to the question of whether one can
establish monotonicity of the spectrum in a more abstract dynamical framework. More
precisely, let (Z, G, τ) be a dynamical system, and let S denote the space of invariant
dynamical subsystems of Z. Can one find a partial order relation ≺on S such that
X ≺Y
=⇒
σ(AX) ⊆σ(AY ),
where (Ax)x∈Z is a dynamically-defined operator family?
A quick but trivial answer would be to define X ≺Y by X ⊆Y , in which case the
above implication holds by definition of the spectrum.
However, the question should
be understood as seeking nontrivial order structures that induce monotonicity of the
associated spectra beyond such elementary inclusions.
A natural starting point is to analyze substitution systems, where the recursive definition
of the approximations via the substitution rule may lead to spectral inclusions.
Spectral estimates beyond operators on lattices
In Example 6.6, we described how to construct a Delone graph associated with a given
Delone set. We observed that the corresponding graph Schr¨odinger operator almost fits
into the framework of dynamically-defined operators, except that it acts on the Hilbert
space ℓ2(D) over the Delone set D, which typically does not possess a group structure.
To study such operators, a natural framework is provided by groupoids, as used in [29].
A central open question is whether the spectral estimates obtained in [28, 41] can be
extended to this more general setting. This requires techniques for comparing operators
acting on different Hilbert spaces, as a change of the Delone set alters the underlying
ℓ2-space. Preliminary investigations with Horia Cornean suggest that such a comparison
might be feasible.
With such tools at hand, one could revisit the question of periodic approximations for
well-known aperiodic tilings such as the octagonal tiling or the Ammann-Beenker tiling
(see Section 3.7). Moreover, there exist many additional substitution-based models to
which the approximation results developed in [19], discussed in Section 3.6, could be
extended.
Improving spectral estimates when gaps do not close
In Chapter 4, we discussed that the spectral map associated with a family of dynamically-
defined operators typically exhibits a square root behavior; see also Remark 8.17. More
precisely, if (Az)z∈Z is a family of self-adjoint operators with Lipschitz continuous kernel,
then Theorem 4.7 (a) implies
dH(σ(AX), σ(AY )) ≤C δH(X, Y )
1
2,


## Page 112


104
CHAPTER 7. OUTLOOK
i.e., although the kernel is Lipschitz continuous, the spectral map is only 1
2-H¨older con-
tinuous. This reduced regularity originates from the possible closing of spectral gaps [27].
More precisely, let (Xn)n∈N ⊆S be a sequence converging to X ∈S in the Hausdorff
topology. A spectral gap of AX is an open interval g = (a, b) ⊆R such that a, b ∈σ(AX)
and (a, b) ∩σ(AX) = ∅. By continuity of the spectrum with respect to the dynamical
system, there exists a sequence of spectral gaps gn = (an, bn) ⊆R of AXn such that
|an −a| ≤C δH(Xn, X)
1
2,
|bn −b| ≤C δH(Xn, X)
1
2.
In particular, limn→∞|an −bn| > 0, so the gaps do not close in the limit.
This raises the natural question whether the above estimates can be improved under the
additional assumption that the limiting gap g = (a, b) remains open. More precisely, can
one prove that
|an −a| ≤˜C δH(Xn, X),
|bn −b| ≤˜C δH(Xn, X),
for some constant ˜C > 0?
This question is motivated by several observations. First, when spectral gaps close (i.e.,
|an −bn| →0), the convergence of the spectrum slows down – a phenomenon exploited in
the analysis of the almost Mathieu operator [205] and the Kohmoto model [31] to show
optimality of the estimate in Theorem 4.7; see also the discussions in Sections 4.3 and
Section 4.5. This reduction in spectral regularity is also discussed in [27].
On the other hand, using C∗-algebraic methods, it was shown in [47] that this square root
behavior disappears if spectral gaps do not close, at least for the rotation algebra including
the almost Mathieu operator. This raises the question of whether such an improvement
is a general phenomenon. In particular, it would be interesting to explore whether the
techniques developed in [28] or [41] can be refined to yield Lipschitz spectral estimates
under the assumption that gaps remain open.
Examples beyond the discrete case
In the discussion following Theorem 4.7, we exclusively considered dynamically-defined
operator families with a discrete underlying group. It is a natural and intriguing question
whether the general framework also extends to continuous settings, since we allow the
underlying group to be non-discrete in general.
To be more specific, let us consider the continuous group G = Rn and the Laplace operator
−∆. Fix parameters r0, R0 > 0 and consider the compact metric space Del(r0, R0) of
Delone sets as introduced in Section 3.2.
Given a continuous potential function V :
Del(r0, R0) →R (which is automatically bounded due to compactness), we define for
each D ∈Del(r0, R0) the Schr¨odinger operator
HD := −∆+ VD,
where the multiplication operator VD is given by
(VDψ)(x) := V (−x + D) ψ(x).
We omit the technical details regarding domains, which can be defined via suitable
sesquilinear forms as in Chapter 6, using a completion of smooth, compactly supported
functions on Rn. Since VD is uniformly bounded in D ∈Del(r0, R0) and −∆is nonnega-
tive, the operator exp(−HD) is well defined and bounded on L2(Rn).


## Page 113


105
By the Dunford-Pettis criterion for kernels, exp(−HD) is an integral operator on Rn, and
hence possesses an integral kernel kD. A direct computation shows that the family of
integral kernels (kD)D∈Del(r0,R0) arises from a bounded kernel k : Rn × Del(r0, R0) →R
defined on the dynamical system (Del(r0, R0), Rn, τ).
Moreover, classical heat kernel
estimates provide quantitative decay bounds on k in Rn.
The open question is whether Lipschitz continuity of the function V : Del(r0, R0) →R
implies that the associated kernel k is also Lipschitz continuous in the sense of Defini-
tion 4.4. If this is the case – or even under a weaker assumption – then Theorem 4.7
would apply to the operator family (exp(−HD))D∈Del(r0,R0), enabling spectral estimates
for the corresponding Schr¨odinger operators.
This approach would also yield local spectral estimates for the original operators HD, see
the related discussion in [26, Sec. 2.5].
The Kohmoto butterfly: Coloring and self-similarity
Following Chapter 5, the resolution of the dry ten Martini Problem (DTMP) for Sturmian
Hamiltonians [18] opens up various further questions. As mentioned earlier, the Kohmoto
butterfly (and similar spectral figures) is numerically generated from periodic approxi-
mations, i.e., for rational values of the frequency parameter. In contrast, the DTMP
addresses the spectral properties of irrational rotations.
In [15], we propose a coloring scheme for the Kohmoto butterfly (see Figure 5.6), as briefly
discussed at the end of Section 5.3. The labeling of spectral gaps for rational parameters
is chosen to be consistent with any continuation of their continued fraction expansion,
yielding a diagram structurally analogous to that of the Hofstadter butterfly [190]. This
construction raises the question of whether a winding number can be associated with
these gap labels. Initial steps in this direction have been made in [142, 143].
The symbolic coding developed by Raymond [207] has been applied in the literature (see
e.g. [147, 177, 86, 92, 176, 93, 69, 179]) to estimate the fractal dimensions of spectra.
Since the coding structure — represented in the spectral tree — is now available for all
coupling constants, a natural question is whether this framework can be used to study
fractal dimension properties more systematically, particularly for small coupling constants.
It is worth noting, however, that existing estimates often rely on further estimations that
also require the coupling constant to be sufficiently large.
As already hinted at in the introduction, these butterfly plots suggest an intrinsic self-
similarity of the underlying spectra.
Proving such self-similarity rigorously remains a
longstanding open problem. In a joint project with Ram Band and Yannik Thomas, we
address this question using the techniques developed in [18, 16]. It would also be of interest
to investigate whether these methods can be extended to other models, most notably
the Hofstadter butterfly. A potential starting point is the nested covering constructed
in [131, 130].

8.1
Weighted Delone sets
In this section, we provide the proofs of the statements presented in Section 3.2. We recall
the relevant notions introduced therein, including weighted Delone sets Del(r0, R0) and
the space U(r0, σ) of r0-uniformly discrete sets (see page 21) defined on a locally compact
second countable (lcsc) group G equipped with an adapted metric dG.
We denote by B(x, r) the open ball of radius r > 0 centered at x ∈G, and write B(r) :=
B(e, r) for the ball centered at the identity element e ∈G. Furthermore, Cc(G) denotes
the space of continuous functions with compact support in G.
For Π := (P, α) ∈U(r0, σ), the weighted dirac comb on the Borel σ-algebra B(G) of G is
defined in eq. (3.3) by
µΠ : B(G) →[0, ∞],

8.1. WEIGHTED DELONE SETS
125
Sending eε to zero implies dwD(Π, Ξ) ≤dwD(Π, Θ) + dwD(Θ, Ξ) as claimed.
dwD induces the weak* topology: Recall that the weak*-topology on the Radon measures
µ on G is the coarsest topology so that µ 7→µ(φ) is continuous for all φ ∈Cc(G). By
definition of dwD it is elementary to see that dwD induces the weak*-topology see also a
detailed elaboration in [35, Prop. B.1]. We refer the reder also to [61] where this topology
is compared to the Chabauty-Fell topology if σ = 1.
(U(r0, σ), dwD) is compact: Following [13, 61], a Radon measure µ on G is called (C, V )-
translation bounded if C > 0, V ⊆G is a relatively compact open subset and
|µ|(gV ) ≤C,
for all g ∈G,
where |µ| denotes the total variation of the measure µ. Then [13, Thm. 2] respectively
[61, Lem. 3.5] imply that the space MC,V (G) of (C, V )-translation bounded measures
(for fixed C and V ) is compact in the weak*-topology. Note that in [13] the group G
is assumed to be a σ-compact locally compact Abelian group. The presented result is
only based on fundamental functional analysis tools and is not using that the group is
Abelian. Using [81, Prop. 2.A.10], we conclude that the group G that we consider is also
σ-compact and so MC,V (G) is compact in our situation.
Set C := σ and V := B(r0) be the open ball of radius r0 around e ∈G. Since dG is a
proper metric V is relatively compact and open. For Π ∈U(r0, σ) and g ∈G, we have
|µΠ|(gV ) ≤C by construction of the measure µΠ and using that the support supp(µΠ)
is r0-uniformly discrete.
Hence, via the identification U(r0, σ) ∋Π 7→µΠ we embed
U(r0, σ) into MC,V (G) and this embedding is a homeomorphism (onto its image) by the
previous considerations stating that U(r0, σ) equipped with the topology inherited from
dwD coincides with the weak*-topology on this embedding. To simplify notation we view
U(r0, σ) as a subset of MC,V (G) via this embedding. Since MC,V (G) is compact, the
metric space (U(r0, σ), dwD) is compact if U(r0, σ) is a closed subset of MC,V (G). For
colored point sets this is proven in [186, Sec. 2.1, Prop. 3.5]. For completeness, we provide
the details here.
Let Πn ∈U(r0, σ), n ∈N, be such that µn := µΠn converges in the weak*-topology to
µ ∈MC,V (G) and we show µ = µΠ for a suitable Π ∈U(r0, σ).
We first prove that supp(µ) is r0-uniformly discrete. Assume towards contradiction that
this is not the case. Thus, there is a g ∈G and x, y ∈B(g, r0) with x, y ∈supp(µ) and
x ̸= y. By Urysohn’s lemma there are two continuous, non-negative functions fx, fy with
supp(fx) ⊆B(g, r0), supp(fy) ⊆B(g, r0), supp(fx)∩supp(fx) = ∅and fx(x) = 1 = fy(y).
Since B(g, r0) ∩supp(µn) is a singleton for each n ∈N, it cannot intersect both supports
of fx and fy simultaneously. Hence, there is no loss of generality in assuming that there
is a subsequence µnk such that µnk(fx) = 0 for all k ∈N. Consequently,
0 < µ(fx) = lim
n→∞µnk(fx) = 0,
a contradiction. Thus, supp(µn) ⊆G is r0-uniformly discrete implying that µ is a dirac
measure.
Define P := supp(µ) and α : supp(µ) →R by α(x) := µ({x}). It is left to show that
the range of α is contained in [σ−1, σ]. Therefore, let x ∈supp(µ) and choose f ∈Cc(G)
such that f(x) = 1, 0 ≤f ≤1 and supp(f) ⊆B(x, r0).
Since µn converges in the
weak*-topology to µ, there exists an n0 ∈N such that µn(f) > 0 for all n ≥n0. Then,
µn(f) ∈[σ−1, σ] for all n ≥n0, lead to
µ({x}) = µ(f) −µn(f) + µn(f) ≤µ(f) −µn(f) + σ

(U(r0, σ), dwD) is a complete metric space: Since (U(r0, σ), dwD) is a compact metric space,
a generalization of the Heine-Borel theorem yields that (U(r0, σ), dwD) is also a complete
metric space.
(Del(r0, R0, σ), dwD) is compact metric space: Since (U(r0, σ), dwD) is a compact metric
space, it suffices to prove that Del(r0, R0, σ) ⊆U(r0, σ) is a closed subset. Let Πn ∈
Del(r0, R0, σ), n ∈N, and Π ∈U(r0, σ) be such that Πn →Π.
We prove that Π ∈
Del(r0, R0, σ), namely supp(Π) ⊆G is R0-relatively dense.

0 ≤f ≤1 and f|B(g,R0) ≡1. For n ∈N, supp(Πn)∩B(g, R0) ̸= ∅since Πn ∈Del(r0, R0, σ)
implying µΠn(f) ≥σ−1. Thus,
0 = µΠ(f) = lim
n→∞µΠn(f) ≥σ−1 > 0
follows, a contradiction.
Next, we prove that this group actions is actually Lipschitz continuous (see Definition 3.1)
stated in Proposition 3.16.
Proposition 8.2. Let G be a lcsc group, r0, R0 > 0 and σ ≥1. Then (U(r0, σ), G, τ)
and (Del(r0, R0, σ), G, τ) are a topological dynamical system. Moreover, the group action
is Lipschitz continuous, namely for all g ∈G and Π, Ξ ∈U(r0, σ), we have
dwD(g.Π, g.Ξ) ≤(dG(e, g) + 1)dwD(Π, Ξ).
Proof. By Proposition 3.15, (U(r0, σ), dwD) and (Del(r0, R0, σ), dwD) are compact metric
spaces. Thus, if we prove the claimed Lipschitz continuity, then the statement follows
using Del(r0, R0, σ) ⊆U(r0, σ).
Let g ∈G and Π, Ξ ∈U(r0, σ). To shorten the notation set |g| := dG(e, g) ≥0 and a :=
