8.2
Substitution systems
Following the discussion in Section 3.4 (see the remark on page 34), a modified notion
of “sufficiently large” is used here and in [19], compared to the version employed in the
original ArXiv manuscript [34] from September 2021.1
The main focus of [34] was to prove the existence of linearly repetitive systems when the
underlying group is no longer abelian. The modified notion introduced here allows one
to incorporate a broader class of examples. For the sake of transparency, we include the
main adjustments required in the proofs from [34] when working with this new notion of
“sufficiently large”. These modifications are also reflected in an updated version of [34].
The notion of “sufficiently large” is introduced to ensure that the supports of iterated
substitution patches eventually cover the entire space in the limit.
More precisely, it
guarantees that the support of an n-fold substitution contains any given compact subset
of the group (up to translation), which in turn ensures that the associated subshift is
non-empty.
To establish linear repetitivity, one further requires that these supports can be bounded
from both inside and outside by balls whose radii grow proportionally to λn
0, where n
denotes the number of iterations and λ0 > 1 is the stretch factor.
As discussed in Section 3.4, the sets V (n) defined in equation (3.7) (see page 33) represent
the support of the n-fold substitution. As illustrated in Example 3.49 (see also [34]), these
supports may exhibit substantially different behavior in the non-Abelian case compared
to the classical Abelian setting.
Recall the setting introduced in Section 3, G is a locally compact, second countable
Hausdorff group (lcsc) with an adapted metric dG. The open ball around x ∈G with
radius r > 0 is denoted by B(x, r) := {y ∈G : dG(x, y) < r}. For a subset F ⊆G and
r > 0, define
F+r :=

To prove the spectral estimates, we use cut-off functions χ. In the following, we first
estimate the corresponding error term arising when commuting a dynamically-defined
operator with such a cut-off function.
Let L∞(G) denote the space of (equivalence classes of) all essentially bounded functions.
In the following, we always identify an element of L∞(G) with a bounded representative
f : G →C , i.e. supg∈G f(g) < ∞.
For 0 ̸= χ ∈L∞(G), define the linear bounded operator (componentwise multiplication

[χ] : HN →HN,
([χ]ψ)n(g) := χ(g)ψn(g).

χj : G →[0, ∞),
χj(g) := χ(j−1g),
χh : G →[0, ∞),
χh(g) := χ(gh).
The commutator of two linear bounded operators A and B, is defined by [A, B] := AB −
BA. We continue with the analog of [41, Lem. 4.2].

Next we formulate and prove the analog of [41, Lem. 4.3], which is the heart of the proof.
Recall the notion of the Hausdorff metric discussed in Chapter 3 (see page 12). The
Hausdorff metric on the compact subsets of C is denoted by dH and is used to measure

Remark 8.15. Lemma 8.14 provides an estimate on the Hausdorff distance between
dynamically-defined operators restricted to different dynamical subsystems, with two main
contributions:
• The first term depends on the regularity of the kernel (i.e., the operator coefficients)
with respect to the second variable Z. Its size increases with the support of χ, as the
group action explores larger regions in Z.
• The second term depends on the choice of the cut-off function χ. It becomes small
if χ approximates an invariant mean, which exists when the group G is amenable.
Such approximations typically require χ to have large support.
Hence, there is a trade-off: small support of χ improves the first term, while large support
improves the second. Balancing these effects leads to the characteristic square-root behav-
ior in the spectral map; see Theorem 8.16. We emphasize that this square-root behavior
is optimal in general and reflects a conceptual insight rather than a technical artifact of
the proof.


## Page 150


142
CHAPTER 8. APPENDIX
Following the previous remark, this is the point where additional assumptions on the
group become necessary – in particular, we require the group to be amenable. In order
to make the upper bound in Lemma 8.14 small, we require the term
∥χh −χ∥2
∥χ∥2
to be small for a suitable choice of χ.
Suppose for the moment that χ is compactly
supported, i.e., there exists a compact set K ⊆G such that χ(g) = 0 for all g /∈K.
Since χh denotes the right shift of χ, this condition expresses that the L2-mass on the
“boundary”, as captured by ∥χh −χ∥2, must be small relative to the total mass ∥χ∥2.
More precisely, a unimodular locally compact second countable (lcsc) group G with Haar
measure mG is called amenable if for every ε > 0 and every compact set K ⊆G, there
exists a bounded function 0 ̸= χ : G →[0, ∞) such that
sup
h∈K
∥χh −χ∥2 < ε∥χ∥2.
In this case, one also says that G satisfies the Reiter condition on L2; see [107, Thm. 10.15].
One natural choice is to define χ as the characteristic function supported on the inverse
of a left-Følner sequence. A sequence (Fn)n∈N of compact subsets Fn ⊆G is called a
(left-)Følner sequence if for every compact subset K ⊆G, we have
lim
n→∞sup
h∈K
mG(hFn∆Fn)
mG(Fn)
= 0,
where hFn∆Fn := (hFn \ Fn) ∪(Fn \ hFn) denotes the symmetric difference of these sets.
For the Lamplighter group (discussed in Section 4.6), this is done; see the proof of Propo-
sition 8.19. The Lamplighter group has exponential growth, and thus balls do not yield
a Følner sequence. However, alternative sets can be employed to construct appropriate
cut-off functions χ.
In [41] and in the following sections, we primarily focus on groups of strict polynomial
growth. These groups are amenable and admit suitable cut-off functions χ supported on
balls; see, for instance, [41, Lem. 4.5] and Equation (8.11) below. Balls in such groups
form a Følner sequence. Note, however, that we do not use characteristic functions on
balls, but rather tent-shaped functions; see Remark 8.17 and Figure 8.3 for an illustration.
In fact, choosing tent-shaped functions optimizes the spectral estimates.
With the previous estimates at hand, one could prove an analog of [41, Thm. 4.4] –
stating the continuity of the spectrum – for N ∈N. We omit this here and only provide
the quantitative estimates. We also note that the continuity of the spectrum follows also
by different methods from [29].
We now strengthen the assumptions of Proposition 8.10 by requiring explicit decay esti-
mates of the off-diagonal entries of the kernel (linear decay) as well as regularity of the
kernel in Z (either Lipschitz continuity or local constancy). These assumptions may be
relaxed depending on the specific model, which then leads to modified spectral estimates.
Recall the notion of Lipschitz continuous dynamical system (Definition 3.1) and strictly
polynomially growing group (Definition 4.6).

Figure 8.3: Different choices for the cut-off function χr supported on the ball B(e, r) are
illustrated. In panel (a), χr is chosen as the characteristic function of the ball, while in
panel (b), it is given by a tent-shaped function.
Remark 8.17. In the final part of the proof (after the first estimate was established),
we observe the interplay between the amenability of the group and the regularity of the
kernel.
The first summand becomes small when the radius is small, while the second
term decreases as the radius grows. The smallness of the second term originates from the
amenability property. This trade-off ultimately results in the characteristic square-root
behavior in the spectral estimates.
Furthermore, we emphasize the importance of the specific choice of the cut-off function χ,
as illustrated by the two alternatives shown in Figure 8.3. For the almost Mathieu operator,
the characteristic function of a ball B(e, r) was initially employed in [78], which led to a
spectral estimate with a 1
3-H¨older behavior. In contrast, the use of a tent function in [8]
resulted in a 1
2-H¨older behavior, consistent with Theorem 8.16. This exponent is in fact
optimal for the almost Mathieu operator, as established in [205]; see also the discussion
in [41]. In light of this, a tent-shaped cut off function is preferable than a characteristic
function.
Next we prove the analog of [41, Thm. 4.7] for general N ∈N.
Theorem 8.18. Let (Z, G, τ) be a dynamical system where G is an unimodular lcsc
group with adapted metric dG. For N ∈N, let k : G × Z →CN×N be a bounded kernel
with dynamically-defined operator (Ax)x∈Z such that Ax is normal for every x ∈Z. If
furthermore

d(gx, gy) ≤(cτdG(e, g) + 1) d(x, y),
g ∈G, x, y ∈Z.

8.4
Spectral estimates for the Lamplighter group
As discussed in Section 4.6, the proof strategy for strictly polynomially growing groups
applies to general amenable groups. For illustration, we prove here spectral estimates for
symbolic dynamical systems over the lamplighter group as stated in Proposition 4.16.
First, recall the definition of the lamplighter group
G :=
(
(m, γ)
 m ∈Z, γ ∈
M
j∈Z
Z2
)
,
where Z2 denotes the cyclic group of order two. The second component γ is a finitely
supported map γ : Z →Z2, since we consider the direct sum.
The group multiplication is defined by
(m, γ)(n, η) := (m + n, Sn(γ) + η) ,
where S : L
j∈Z Z2 →L
j∈Z Z2 is the shift operator given by S(γ)(j) := γ(j + 1) for all
j ∈Z.
The inverse of an element in G is given by
(m, γ)−1 =
 −m, S−m(γ)

.
The set
S = {t, at} = {(1, 0), (1, δ1)}
generates the lamplighter group, where t = (1, 0) and a = (0, δ0). Here, δj : Z →Z2
denotes the sequence that vanishes everywhere except at the jth position, where it is one.
A generating set S can be used to define the word metric on G, namely, dG(g, h) is given
by the minimal number m ∈N0 of elements sj ∈S ∪S−1 such that
g−1h = s1 · · · sm,


## Page 155


8.4. SPECTRAL ESTIMATES FOR THE LAMPLIGHTER GROUP
147
with the convention that m = 0 if g−1h = e. Note that the word metric can also be
interpreted as the shortest-path metric on the Cayley graph of G with respect to the
generating set S. It is straightforward to verify that every word metric is adapted on a
discrete group G.
In the following, we work with the word metric dG defined by the generating set S =
{t, at}. This word metric is studied and computed in [139, Sec. 2].
We now consider the symbolic dynamical system (AG, G, τ) (see Example 3.3) for the
lamplighter group and a finite alphabet A. Recall from Section 3.2.2 that the metric dG
induces a metric dA on AG. Moreover, dA induces the Hausdorff metric δA on the set S
of invariant, closed, and non-empty subsets.
We now restate Proposition 4.16 and proceed with its proof.
Proposition 8.19. Let (AG, G, τ) be a symbolic dynamical system for a finite alphabet
A where G is the lamplighter group equipped with the word metric dG defined by the
generating set S = {t, at}. For N ∈N, let k : G × Z →CN×N be a bounded kernel
with dynamically-defined operator (Ax)x∈Z such that Ax is normal for every x ∈Z. If

Proof. Since G is discrete, G itself is a Delone set of G (for suitable parameters) and so
the symbolic dynamical system (AG, G, τ) is Lipschitz continuous by Proposition 3.27 (b).
Thus, there exists cτ > 0 such that
dA(gx, gy) ≤(cτdG(e, g) + 1) dA(x, y),

We note at this point that the metric dA on AG depends on the choice of the metric dG,
since the latter determines the shape of balls in G; see Section 3.2.2.
